L30: ## I. Executive Summary
L31: ## 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L32: Union Chimique Belge (UCB) submitted a new drug application (NDA) 216834 for zilucoplan for the treatment of adults with generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG) who are anti-acetylcholine receptor (AChR) positive. Zilucoplan is a macrocyclic peptide that functions as C5 complement inhibitor by blocking the cleavage of complement C5 to C5a and C5b and inhibiting the formation of membrane attack complex.
L33: NDA 216834 was reviewed by a multidisciplinary review team that did not identify any issues that preclude approval. Each discipline has recommended approval. I, the signatory authority for this application, concur with those recommendations and agree that the benefit-risk assessment supports approval.
L34: The Applicant submitted results from a single adequate and well-controlled study (Study MG0010), with confirmatory evidence from pharmacodynamic/mechanistic data, that provide substantial evidence of effectiveness of zilucoplan for the indication which supports approval for the treatment of adults with gMG who are anti-AChR-positive.
L35: Study MG0010 was a double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter study that provided reliable and highly statistically significant (p&lt;0.001) evidence of an important patient-reported impact and clinical benefit on daily function on an acceptable primary outcome (i.e., change from baseline to week 12 on the Myasthenia Gravis-Specific Activities of Daily Living [MG-ADL]). Efficacy was further supported by a highly statistically significant result (p&lt;0.001) on a secondary endpoint using a validated direct physician assessment of motor function in subjects with gMG (change from baseline on the Quantitative Myasthenia Gravis [QMG] score). Supplementary analyses supported the primary and secondary results. Overall, the highly persuasive results on a clinically meaningful endpoint, with support on the secondary endpoint of QMG and multiple supplementary analyses, make reliance on a single efficacy study in this rare disease, with confirmatory evidence from relevant pharmacodynamic/mechanistic data, appropriate to support approval.
L36: The available safety data show that risks of zilucoplan are acceptable for its intended use. I concur that identified risks can be mitigated through the proposed risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS), product labeling, and be can further evaluated during routine and enhanced pharmacovigilance. The overall benefit-risk is favorable as described in the benefit-risk framework below. For detailed information supporting the basis for this approval, please refer to the detailed reviews included in this interdisciplinary assessment document and the product quality review.
L37: ## 2. Benefit-Risk Assessment
L38: ## 2.1. Benefit-Risk Framework
L39: Table 2. Benefit-Risk Framework
L40: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Analysis of condition	Myasthenia gravis (MG) is a rare autoimmune disease affecting the neuromuscular junction. Abnormalities at the neuromuscular junction result in muscular weakness. At disease onset, some patients experience isolated ptosis and diplopia known as ocular myasthenia gravis. Patients with generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG) experience weakness in other distributions such as bulbar, axial, limb, and respiratory muscles. This weakness can affect activities of daily living and may affect respiration. The disease course is variable, with some patients experiencing prolonged remission while others may experience many emergency room visits.
MG is caused by antibodies that bind to functional proteins at the neuromuscular junction. A large majority of patients have detectable antibodies against the acetylcholine receptor (AChR; 80% of patients), whereas less than 10% of patients have antibodies against muscle specific kinase (MuSK). Antibodies are not detected in 10 to 15% of patients.
The diagnosis of MG is based on the clinical presentation, the presence of autoantibodies (anti-AChR, anti-MuSK, and others), and neurophysiological tests such as repetitive nerve stimulation and single-fiber electromyography.
MG is a rare, serious, life-threatening disease that can cause disability due to muscle weakness and death due to respiratory failure. Different autoantibodies can result in different subgroups of myasthenia gravis with variable phenotypes and severity.
L41: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Current treatment options	There are several FDA-approved drugs for gMG—pyridostigmine, eculizumab (SOLIRIS), ravulizumab (ULTOMIRIS), efgartigimod (VYVGART), and rozanolixizumab (RYSTIGGO).
Pyridostigmine is administered as needed, and is sufficient treatment for some patients. Adverse effects of pyridostigmine are secondary to acetylcholine (ACh) accumulation at muscarinic receptors in smooth muscle and autonomic glands, and at nicotinic receptors in skeletal muscle. Adverse effects include muscular weakness, gastrointestinal side effects (common), and excess bronchial and oral secretions that can be a serious problem in certain patients.
Immunosuppressive treatments such as prednisone, azathioprine, mycophenolate mofetil, tacrolimus, rituximab, and plasmapheresis and intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIg) are used off-label.
The biologic C5 inhibitors (eculizumab and ravulizumab) are FDA approved to treat AChR-positive gMG. These products have a warning and REMS for infections with encapsulated organisms including Neisseria meningitidis and other species.
Efgartigimod, a neonatal Fc receptor blocker, is indicated in AChR-positive gMG. Rozanolixizumab is also a neonatal Fc receptor blocker, and is indicated for the treatment of AChR-positive and MuSK-positive gMG.
Short-term rescue treatments for gMG include plasma exchange and intravenous immune globulin. Thymectomy is indicated for all patients with thymoma and for those with nonthymomatous generalized AChR-positive MG.
Despite currently available therapies, there remains a significant unmet clinical need for effective treatments for MG because not all patients with MG are able to receive, tolerate, or adequately benefit from the currently available clinical treatments.
Corticosteroids, broadly recognized as a mainstay therapy for MG have a rapid action, are inexpensive, and usually result in benefit to patients with MG. However, patients often develop intolerable side effects. Nonsteroidal immunosuppressant treatments may have a delayed response and significant side effects. Many patients have inadequate response to steroids and nonsteroidal immunosuppressant treatment, with persistent and disabling muscle weakness, and disease relapses which may progress to MG crisis. Plasma exchange and intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIg) are used in patients with acute severe MG as their effect is rapid but short-lived.
L42: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	Study MG0010 was a Phase 3, multicenter, randomized (1:1), double-blind, placebo-controlled, 12-week trial of the 0.3 mg/kg daily dose versus placebo in 174 subjects with AChR-positive gMG. The primary endpoint was change from baseline in total Myasthenia Gravis-Specific Activities of Daily Living (MG-ADL) score (8-item provider-administered PRO measure with each item scored 0 to 3, and higher scores indicate more severe symptoms). After 12 weeks of treatment, the between-groups difference in MG-ADL score was -2.09 points (p<0.001) in favor of zilucoplan. For the secondary endpoint, Quantitative Myasthenia Gravis (QMg) score, the difference was -2.94 points (p<0.001) in favor of zilucoplan. The other secondary endpoints (MGC and MG-QOL15r) were consistent with the primary endpoint result.
Study MG0009 was Phase 2, double-blind, placebo-controlled, 12-week trial in 44 subjects with AChR-positive gMG who were randomized (1:1:1) to placebo, 0.1 mg/kg, or 0.3 mg/kg. The primary endpoint was the between-groups difference in change from baseline to 12 weeks in QMG score (13-item validated physician-reported outcome measure with each item graded 0 to 3, and higher scores indicate greater weakness). The primary endpoint trended in favor of zilucoplan.
This small molecule C5 inhibitor has a similar mechanism of action as the biologics eculizumab and ravulizumab, which have shown clinical efficacy in the gMG population.
Study MG0010, a 12-week randomized double-blind, placebo-controlled study of zilucoplan provided reliable and statistically persuasive evidence that zilucoplan can help patients with anti-AChR antibody positive gMG achieve a clinically meaningful improvement in the symptoms of the disease that affect their activities of daily living, motor function, and disease severity.
The potential role of zilucoplan for the treatment of patients with anti-MuSK gMG, seronegative gMG, and those with purely ocular MG is unclear because these patients were not evaluated in the clinical studies included in this BLA submission.
L43: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Risk and risk management	There were 213 subjects with gMG and anti-AChR-positive who were exposed to any dose of zilucoplan. Total exposure (as of datalock) was 254 pt-yrs (329 pt-yrs including 120-day update) for the 0.3 mg/kg zilucoplan dose. A total of 131 subjects with gMG had at least 12 months of exposure to the 0.3 mg/kg zilucoplan dose, including the 120-day update.
The approved C5 inhibitors have a boxed warning and a REMS regarding the risk of meningococcal infection. This adverse event did not occur in the studies of zilucoplan (330 pt-yrs on 0.3 mg/kg) likely due to the small population size and short trial duration. However, a boxed warning will be included in the product labeling for zilucoplan as this risk is a class effect due to the mechanism of action of the drug.
Elevations in pancreatic enzymes and microscopic changes in the pancreas (acinar cell degeneration, fibrosis/fibroplasia, duct degeneration/regeneration, inflammatory cell infiltration) were consistent findings in the toxicity studies conducted in cynomolgus monkeys.
Pancreatitis occurred in the long-term human studies at a higher incidence than in the general population. Pancreatic cysts/pseudocysts with or without infection also occurred. There were two deaths from pancreatic adenocarcinoma. Patients and physicians should be informed of the risk of pancreatitis and other pancreatic conditions while on zilucoplan, which may be life-threatening.
As with the other C5 inhibitors, the risk of meningococcal infections will be addressed in labeling and mitigated through a REMS with recommendations for vaccination and antibiotic prophylaxis.
There was also a safety signal identified for exocrine pancreas-related events for zilucoplan. This risk can be appropriately mitigated through labeling and monitored with enhanced pharmacovigilance.
L44: Abbreviations: Ach = acetylcholine; AChR = acetylcholine receptor; gMG = generalized myasthenia gravis; MG = myasthenia gravis; MG-ADL = Myasthenia Gravis Activities of Daily Living; PRO = patient recorded outcome; QMG = quantitative myasthenia gravis; REMS = Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy
L45: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L46: Myasthenia gravis (MG) is a serious, life-threatening disease that can result in significant morbidity and mortality. Although there are several treatments approved to treat patients with generalized MG (gMG) (i.e., not purely ocular MG), there remains a need for effective treatments because not all patients with gMG are able to receive, tolerate, or adequately benefit from the currently available clinical treatments. Currently, there are two FDA-approved complement component 5 (C5) inhibitors (eculizumab and ravulizumab) for the treatment of adult patients with gMG who are anti-acetylcholine receptor (AChR) antibody positive. There are two FDA-approved neonatal Fc receptor blockers (efgartigimod and rozanolixizumab). Efgartigimod is approved for intravenous administration and efgartigimod with hyaluronidase is approved for subcutaneous administration for the treatment of adult patients with gMG who are AChR-antibody positive. Rozanolixizumab is approved for the treatment of gMG in adult patients who are AChR or anti-muscle-specific tyrosine kinase (MuSK) antibody positive.
L47: The Applicant has demonstrated the efficacy of zilucoplan for the treatment of adults with gMG who are anti-AChR-antibody positive in study MG0010. This study was a randomized double-blind, placebo-controlled, multicenter study that enrolled 200 subjects with gMG who were on stable dose of acetylcholinesterase inhibitors, corticosteroids, and/or nonsteroidal immunosuppressive drugs. Subjects received 12 weeks of daily subcutaneous zilucoplan at a dose of 0.3 mg/kg (based on a bodyweight dosing table) or matching placebo. The primary efficacy endpoint was the between-groups difference in change in Myasthenia Gravis-Specific Activities of Daily Living (MG-ADL) total score (patient-reported outcome [PRO]) between baseline and week 12. The key secondary endpoints were Quantitative Myasthenia Gravis (QMG), myasthenia gravis composite (MGC), and Revised Myasthenia Gravis Quality of Life 15-Item Scale (MG-QOL15r). The Applicant assessed the between-groups difference in change from baseline to Week 12. This primary (MG-ADL) and secondary endpoint (QMG) have been used previously to support the approvals of efgartigimod, ravulizumab, eculizumab, and rozanolixizumab.
L48: The Applicant has demonstrated a statistically significant and clinically meaningful effect on the primary and key secondary endpoints. There was an improvement (decrease) in the MG-ADL total score by 4.39 points (least squares mean [LSM] estimate) in the zilucoplan group as compared to 2.3 points in the placebo group (p&lt;0.001). Efficacy was supported by consistent directional changes in the key secondary endpoints, where QMG score decreased (improved) over 12 weeks by 6.19 points as compared to 3.25 points for placebo (p&lt;0.001). MGC and MG-QOL15r also showed similar trends in favor of zilucoplan.
L49: The Applicant seeks an indication for the treatment of adults with gMG who are AChR-antibody positive. Study MG0010 was an adequate and well-controlled, multicenter study that provided reliable and persuasive evidence that zilucoplan benefited adult patients with gMG who were anti-AChR-antibody positive to achieve a clinically meaningful improvement in the symptoms of MG that affect their activities of daily living. Consistent and distinct treatment effects on MG-ADL and QMG confirm that the observed treatment differences between zilucoplan and placebo were clinically and statistically meaningful.
L50: Study MG0010 was relatively large, given the size of the patient population. No single site provided an unusually large proportion of subjects, and no site was disproportionately
L51: responsible for the observed treatment effect. These findings were statistically robust to sensitivity analyses and were generalizable across demographic subgroups of age, sex, body weight, and region of the world.
L52: Additionally, pharmacodynamic and mechanistic data relevant to the pathophysiology of MG—increased mean total C5 levels (due to prolongation of half-life of zilucoplan-bound C5 rather than free C5) in the zilucoplan group as compared to no change in the placebo group—provide confirmatory evidence of near complete inhibition of C5 to support approval. Therefore, study MG0010 is capable of serving as a single adequate and well-controlled clinical study plus confirmatory evidence to establish substantial evidence of effectiveness of zilucoplan treatment in adult patients with gMG who are AChR-antibody positive.
L53: The safety database for zilucoplan was adequate for the intended population and proposed dosing regimen. The safety database for gMG consisted of three studies—MG0009 (double-blind followed by an extension phase), MG0010 (double-blind only), and MG0011 (long-term extension of MG0009 and MG0010). At the 0.3 mg/kg zilucoplan dose level, 131 subjects with gMG were exposed for at least one year, including the 120-day update. Common adverse events included injection site reactions, upper respiratory tract infection, urinary tract infection, diarrhea, nausea, vomiting, and increased lipase and amylase.
L54: Pancreatitis and other effects of zilucoplan such as pancreatic cysts and pseudocysts with and without infection were seen in the human studies of zilucoplan. This was consistent with the histologic findings and lipase (and amylase) elevations seen in the nonclinical studies. Additionally, two subjects died two months after diagnosis of advanced pancreatic adenocarcinoma (1 with history of pancreatitis and the other without).
L55: These safety findings can be adequately addressed through labeling and through routine and enhanced pharmacovigilance and a REMS. Warning and Precautions statements will include serious meningococcal infection, other infection with encapsulated bacteria, and pancreatitis and other pancreatic conditions. Enhanced pharmacovigilance will be requested for serious adverse events related to infections, including meningococcal infection and opportunistic infection, and pancreatic conditions. A pregnancy outcomes study and a lactation study will be required as postmarketing requirements.
L56: Based on the review of all available efficacy and safety data, the benefits of zilucoplan outweigh the risks for the treatment of adult patients with gMG who are AChR-antibody positive. The availability of zilucoplan will provide a new and effective treatment option via daily subcutaneous injections of this C5 inhibitor.
L57: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L58: ## 3. Introduction
L59: Myasthenia gravis is a rare antibody-mediated autoimmune neuromuscular disease. Patients with myasthenia gravis can be classified according to clinical features of the disease. Approximately two-thirds of patients will present with diplopia and ptosis and other symptoms restricted to the ocular muscles, referred to as ocular myasthenia gravis. Patients with generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG) experience weakness in other distributions such as bulbar, axial, limb, and respiratory muscles. Most patients with gMG have detectable antibodies against the acetylcholine receptor (AChR).
L60: There are several FDA-approved drugs for gMG — pyridostigmine, eculizumab, ravulizumab, efgartigimod, and rozanolixizumab. Other options include immunosuppressive treatment (off-label) such as steroids and nonsteroidal immunosuppressants, and plasma exchange or intravenous immunoglobulin therapy (also off-label). However, patients often experience intolerable side effects. Up to 30% of patients have inadequate response to steroids and nonsteroidal immunosuppressant treatment, with persistent and disabling muscle weakness. There remains a significant unmet clinical need for effective treatments for gMG because not all patients are able to receive, tolerate, or adequately benefit from the available clinical treatments.
L61: Zilucoplan is an inhibitor of complement C5-convertase that prevents the generation of anaphylatoxin C5a and the membrane attack complex. There are two approved C5 inhibitors for the treatment of gMG. Unlike zilucoplan, which is a peptide, these approved C5 inhibitors are monoclonal antibodies.
L62: The proposed dosing regimen of zilucoplan is subcutaneous once daily dose of 0.3 mg/kg, which is supplied as prefilled syringes of various colors according to the dose provided. The proposed dosing, and that used in the studies, was according to bodyweight —16.6 mg (43 kg to &lt;56 kg; rubine red), 23.0 mg (56 to &lt;77 kg; orange), and 32.4 mg (77 to &lt;150 kg; dark blue).
L63: The Applicant seeks approval of zilucoplan for the treatment of adults with acetylcholine receptor antibody positive generalized myasthenia gravis. The Applicant has submitted the results of study MG0010 in support of the application. Study MG0010 was a 12-week, randomized (1:1), double-blind, placebo-controlled study designed to demonstrate superiority of zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg in a population of 174 subjects with AChR-positive gMG. The Applicant also submitted the results of a Phase 2 study, MG0009 with a similar design. Study MG0009 included 44 subjects randomized (1:1:1) into 3 dose arms (zilucoplan 0.1 mg/kg, 0.3mg/kg, or placebo), followed by an extension phase. Subjects from both studies continued treatment in an open-label extension, study MG0011.
L64: ## 3.1. Review Issue List
L65: ## 3.1.1. Key Efficacy Review Issues
L66: Not applicable.
L67: ## 3.1.2. Key Safety Review Issues
L68: The key safety review issues for this application are meningococcal infection (a class effect of the C5 inhibitors), other serious infections, and pancreatitis, which was seen in the human as well as nonclinical studies.
L69: ## 3.1.2.1. Pancreatitis and Associated Pancreatic Conditions
L70: Pancreatitis and other effects of zilucoplan on the exocrine pancreas were seen in the human studies of zilucoplan. This was consistent with the histologic findings and lipase (and amylase) elevations seen in the nonclinical studies.
L71: Seven subjects experienced pancreatic adverse events (four pancreatitis and multiple pancreatic cysts) in the extension phases. Five of these seven subjects had no prior pancreatic history. There were two deaths from pancreatic cancer. One subject who died from pancreatic cancer had a history of a previous pancreatic condition and the other did not.
L72: Adverse events of elevated lipase and amylase were also noted in the clinical studies. There were no dose-modification criteria in these protocols for pancreatic events, despite lipase levels being scheduled lab tests in these studies due to the nonclinical findings of increased lipase. There was no requirement for baseline pancreatic ultrasound. Consequently, a few investigators considered pancreatic events related to zilucoplan, but most did not. This led to variability in discontinuation of zilucoplan due to pancreatic events. No subjects with pancreatic adverse events had zilucoplan discontinued permanently (except the two subjects who died shortly after the diagnosis of pancreatic cancer), and most subjects had zilucoplan continued through their pancreatic episodes.
L73: Refer to Section 7.7.1 for further details.
L74: ## 3.1.2.2. Meningococcal Infection
L75: Patients treated with complement inhibitors are at increased risk of *Neisseria* infections, particularly *Neisseria meningitidis*. The currently approved products have a boxed warning and a REMS to mitigate this risk.
L76: No meningococcal infections were reported in the submitted studies of zilucoplan. However, the studies were of relatively small size and short duration. Subjects were required by protocol to have been vaccinated. Refer to Section 7.7.2 for further details.
L77: ## 3.1.2.3. Other Serious Infections
L78: Refer to Section 7.7.3 for further details.

Results from Phase 3 study MG0010 provide the primary basis for efficacy and safety assessment, augmented by the Phase 2 dose-ranging study MG0009 and long-term open-label study MG0011.

L88: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L89: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L90: Zilucoplan is a complement inhibitor, which acts by binding to complement component C5 to inhibit its cleavage to C5a and C5b and thereby inhibiting the formation of the membrane attack complex (MAC, terminal complement complex C5b-9). MAC is thought to mediate tissue damage at the neuromuscular junction in patients with AChR-ab positive myasthenia gravis.
L91: In vitro pharmacology studies demonstrated potent binding of zilucoplan to human complement C5 protein and inhibition of C5 cleavage. In a functional assay, it was shown that binding to C5 and inhibition of C5 cleavage by zilucoplan blocked red blood cell lysis. An in vitro study
L92: examining inhibition of complement-mediated hemolysis by zilucoplan showed that potency in the cynomolgus monkey (IC50 4 nM) was comparable to that in humans (IC50 7 nM) but much reduced in rat (609 nM) and other species (dog &gt;4700 nM, mouse &gt;36,000 nM, rabbit &gt;67,000 nM, and guinea pig &gt;100,000 nM). In vivo pharmacology studies in cynomolgus monkeys showed that plasma drug levels \ge 2.5 \mu\text{g/mL} produced &gt;90% inhibition of hemolysis. In the repeated-dose toxicity studies in monkey, nearly complete inhibition of hemolysis was demonstrated throughout the dosing period with recovery after dosing cessation.
L93: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
L94: The clinical pharmacology program supporting the marketing application of zilucoplan for gMG indication included a total of nine studies. Six of the studies were Phase 1 studies (Table 5) in healthy volunteers characterizing pharmacokinetic (PK) and pharmacodynamic (PD) [total C5 levels, and complement inhibition as measured by sheep red blood cell (sRBC) lysis] of zilucoplan and evaluating the impact of intrinsic and extrinsic factors (Section 8.1 and 8.2). Three of the studies are Phase 2 study (MG0009) and two Phase 3 studies (MG0010 and MG0011 [open-label extension of MG0009 and MG0010]) in gMG subjects in which PK and PD data of zilucoplan were collected. The major clinical pharmacology review focus is the assessment of the mechanistic evidence based on PD endpoint data (total C5 levels and complement inhibition) in gMG subjects (Phase 2 and 3 studies; Section 6.1.2) and the assessment of the proposed body weight-band based dosing for zilucoplan (Section 8.1.4).

L98: **Table 6. Summary of Clinical Pharmacology and Pharmacokinetics**
L99: Characteristic	Drug Information
Pharmacologic Activity
Established pharmacologic class (EPC)	Zilucoplan is 15-amino acid macrocyclic peptide that is a complement component 5 (C5) inhibitor.
Mechanism of action	Zilucoplan inhibits the effects of C5 through a dual mechanism of action. Specifically, zilucoplan binds with high affinity to C5 and inhibits its cleavage by the C5 convertase to C5a and C5b, which results into downregulation of the assembly and cytolytic activity of the membrane attack complex (MAC). Additionally, by binding to the C5b moiety of C5, zilucoplan sterically hinders binding of C5b to C6 which prevents the subsequent assembly and activity of the MAC, should any C5b be formed.
Active moieties	Zilucoplan
QT prolongation	Based on a thorough-QT study (TQT study, # UP0093) conducted to evaluate the effects of supratherapeutic zilucoplan plasma concentrations on the heart rate (HR)-QTc interval, at two times the recommended dose (i.e., 2x0.3 mg/kg =0.6 mg/kg), zilucoplan does not prolong the QT interval to any clinically relevant extent. The mean (upper 90% confidence interval) QTcF increase was 3.2 milliseconds (5.3 milliseconds).
General Information
Bioanalysis	The plasma, urine, and feces concentration of zilucoplan and metabolites were determined by validated liquid chromatography-mass spectrometry (LC/MS/MS) methods. The total C5 levels and complement inhibition were measured by validated enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay (ELISA) and sheep red blood cell (sRBC) lysis assay respectively. Refer to Section 14.3 for further details.
Healthy subjects versus patients	Based on population pharmacokinetic (PK) analysis, zilucoplan PK is similar in healthy subjects and subjects with gMG.
Drug exposure at steady state following the therapeutic dosing regimen (or single dose, if more relevant for the drug)	From Phase 3 study, MG0010, that dosed 0.3 mg/kg zilucoplan once daily for 12-weeks, mean (SD) Ctrough (ng/mL) were reported to be 12846.9 (2805.3).
Range of effective dose(s) or exposure	A zilucoplan dose of 0.3 mg/kg administered subcutaneously once daily was studied in Phase 3 study (Study # MG0010) that resulted in complement inhibition of 97.5% observed by the end of the first week and sustained throughout the 12-week treatment period.
Maximally tolerated dose or exposure	The highest evaluated dose in humans was 0.6 mg/kg (once daily for 7 days) in adult healthy subjects (n=32) (TQT study, # UP0093)
Dose proportionality	Zilucoplan peak exposure (Cmax) was approximately dose-proportional while the increase in zilucoplan. Systemic exposure (AUC0-inf) was less than dose-proportional when administered as a single dose in the range of 0.05 mg/kg to 0.4 mg/kg (Study # UP0112).

## Zilucoplan
L101: Characteristic	Drug Information
Accumulation	Following daily subcutaneous dosing of zilucoplan, 0.3 mg/kg, for 14 days in healthy subjects, both C_{max} and AUC_{tau} increased by approximately 3-fold (Study # UP0113)
Time to achieve steady-state	Steady-state exposures of zilucoplan was achieved by week 4 and remained stable through Week 12 following zilucoplan once daily dose of 0.3 mg/kg for 12 weeks in gMG subjects (Study # MG0010)
Bridge between to-be-marketed and clinical trial/study formulations	To-be-marketed product in three dose presentations (16.6 mg/0.416 mL, 23 mg/0.574mL, 32.4 mg/0.810mL) is a sterile, preservative-free solution (40 mg/mL) in a single-use prefilled syringe placed into a safety syringe device and was also used in Phase 2 and Phase 3 clinical studies. There was no change in the formulation in the clinical development program of zilucoplan. For Phase 1 study # UP0112, zilucoplan formulation was filled in glass vial. Overall, any bridging study is not required.

Bioavailability	Absolute or relative bioavailability of zilucoplan in humans in not studied.
T_{max}	3 – 6 hours (Median)
Food effect (fed/fasted)	Zilucoplan is administered by subcutaneous injection, and therefore food-effect is not relevant.
Geometric least square mean and 90% CI	
Distribution
Volume of distribution	Based on population PK analyses, the mean estimate of volume of distribution at steady state was 3.51 L
Plasma protein binding	>99% protein binding for zilucoplan and its two metabolites, RA102758 and RA103488.
Drug as substrate of transporters	Zilucoplan is not a substrate of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, or OATP1B3. The substrate potential of zilucoplan for renal transporters (OAT1, OAT3, MATE1/MATE2-K, OCT2) is not evaluated. It is noted that excretion of zilucoplan and its metabolites is negligible (<1% of the dose) and hence the lack of these data are not concerning.
Elimination
Mass balance results	No dedicated mass balance study was conducted. In renal impairment study (# UP0114) at a single dose of 0.3 mg/kg, zilucoplan and its metabolites are minimally excreted in urine in both the normal and severe renal impairment subjects (<1% of dose in total across all analytes). Based on the concentrations of zilucoplan in feces from hepatic impairment study (UP0094) at a single dose of 0.3 mg/kg, estimated fecal excretion of zilucoplan and its metabolites was negligible (<1% of dose) in both the normal and moderate hepatic impairment subjects.
Clearance	Based on population PK analyses, the mean estimate of clearance at steady state was 0.085 L/h
Half-life	Mean terminal half-life was 172 hours
L102: Characteristic	Drug Information
Metabolic pathway(s)	
As a peptide, zilucoplan is expected to be degraded into small peptides and amino acids via catabolic pathways. Two primary metabolites of zilucoplan detected in plasma are RA102758 and RA103488. The metabolite RA102758 is formed by protease mediated degradation of the c terminal lysine residue attached to the ethylene glycol-palmitoyl tail. The metabolite RA103488 is formed by \omega-hydroxylation on the palmitoyl tail of intact zilucoplan majorly by CYP4F2 (minor contribution by CYP4A11, CYP4F3A, and CYP4F3B). Of the above two metabolites, RA103488 is pharmacologically active with IC_{50} for %RBC lysis similar to that of zilucoplan (RA103488 – 5.5nM; Zilucoplan – 5.2nM).
Based on study # UP0113 at zilucoplan dose of 0.3 mg/kg (once daily dose for 14 days), the exposure of above two metabolites is low compared to zilucoplan (RA102758 – 10.6% of zilucoplan exposure; RA103488 – 9.4% of zilucoplan exposure). Due to the low exposure of metabolites, any contribution by active metabolite to overall pharmacological activity is therefore not substantial.

Primary excretion pathways (% dose)	
In vitro studies did not suggest any contribution by transporters towards disposition of zilucoplan.
Zilucoplan is a cyclic peptide and is expected to be degraded into small peptides and amino acids via catabolic pathways.
Excretion of zilucoplan and its metabolite in urine and feces was negligible (<1% of the dose).

Body weight	
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight-band based dosing regimen was evaluated throughout the clinical development program for zilucoplan. In Phase 3 study, MG0010, gMG subjects received 0.3 mg/kg once daily doses as per the weight-bands presented in Table 7 which is same as the originally proposed weight-band based dosing regimen. In response to FDA's labeling edits (updated label dated August 14, 2023, sent by email), the Applicant proposed to include patients weighing >150kg in the label (Table 8).

L109: ## NDA 216834
## Zilucoplan
L110: Characteristic	Drug Information
Age	Based on population PK analysis, it was confirmed that body weight is a significant covariate, and the simulation results further support body weight-band based dosing regimen. For details, refer Section 8.1.4 and 14.5.
No age-based dose adjustments are needed. Population PK analyses of data from Phase 1, 2 and 3 studies indicated that age range from 20 to 76 years had no clinically significant impact on the pharmacokinetics of zilucoplan.
Sex	No sex-based dose adjustments are needed. Population PK analyses of data from Phase 1, 2 and 3 studies indicated that sex had no clinically significant impact on the pharmacokinetics of zilucoplan.
Race	No race-based dose adjustments are needed. Population PK analyses of data from Phase 1, 2 and 3 studies indicated that race had no clinically significant impact on the pharmacokinetics of zilucoplan.
Renal impairment	No dose adjustment is needed for patients with any degree of renal impairment based on the findings from clinical study (# UP0114) that compared zilucoplan PK in subjects with severe renal impairment and matched normal healthy subjects.
Hepatic impairment	No dose adjustment is needed in patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment based on the findings from clinical study (# UP0094) that compared zilucoplan PK in subjects with mild and moderate hepatic impairment and matched normal healthy subjects. The pharmacokinetics of zilucoplan in patients with severe hepatic impairment have not been studied.
Inhibition/induction of metabolism	Drug Interaction Liability (Drug as Perpetrator)
Zilucoplan and metabolite RA102758 do not inhibit CYP enzymes (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6, CYP3A, CYP4F).
Zilucoplan does not induce CYP enzymes (CYP1A2, CYP2B6, CYP3A4) and does not inhibit UGT (UGT1A1, UGT1A3, UGT1A4, UGT1A6, UGT1A9, UGT2B7, UGT2B15).
Inhibition/induction of transporter systems	Zilucoplan and metabolite RA102758 do not inhibit transporters (p-gp, BCRP, MATE1, MATE2-K, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, OCT1, OCT2).
Bioanalysis	Immunogenicity (if Applicable)
Immunogenicity assessments were conducted using direct immune binding assay to determine the exposures of antidrug antibodies (ADA) and anti-polyethylene glycol (PEG) antibodies (APA).
Incidence	In Phase 3 study # MG0010, in up to 12 weeks of treatment, up to 2.3% (2/86) and 9.3% (8/86) of subjects treated with zilucoplan developed antidrug antibodies and anti-PEG antibodies respectively.
Clinical impact	Because of the low occurrence of antidrug antibodies and anti-PEG antibodies, the available data are too limited to make definitive conclusions regarding immunogenicity and its effect on pharmacokinetics, pharmacodynamics, safety, or efficacy of zilucoplan.
L111: Source: Reviewer Generated Table
L112: Abbreviations: C5 = complement component 5; MAC = membrane attack complex; gMG = generalized myasthenia gravis; PK = pharmacokinetic; QTc = corrected QT; QTcF = QT corrected for heart rate by Fridericia's cube root formula; TQT = thorough-QT study; Cmax = maximum plasma concentration or peak exposure; AUC0-inf = area under the curve0-inf or total systemic exposure; CYP = cytochrome P450; UGT = UDP glucuronosyl transferase; P-gp = P-glycoprotein; BCRP = breast cancer resistance protein; OATP1B1/1B3 = organic anion transporting polypeptide 1B1/1B3; OAT1/OAT3 = organic anion transporter 1/3; MATE1/MATE2-K = multidrug and toxin extrusion (MATE) proteins; OCT1/OCT2 = organic cation transporter 1/2; PEG = polyethylene glycol
L113: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L114: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L115: ## 6.1.1. Assessment of Dose
L116: For the treatment of gMG in adult patients who are anti-acetylcholine receptor (AChR) antibody positive, the proposed dose for zilucoplan is 0.3 mg/kg once daily administered as a subcutaneous injection and is dependent on actual body weight range as presented in Table 8. Each dose presentation in prefilled syringe provides a fixed dose of 16.6 mg, 23 mg, and 32.4 mg zilucoplan depending on body weight. The dosing regimen using weight brackets (Table 7) was evaluated in Phase 3 (MG0010) study in gMG subjects who are AChR-antibody positive. A body weight-band based dosing regimen was evaluated throughout the clinical development program for zilucoplan. In response to FDA's labeling edits (updated label dated August 14, 2023, sent by email), the Applicant proposed to include patients weighing &gt;150kg in the label (Table 8). Specifically, the dosing for patients with weight &gt;150kg is 32.4mg which is same as the dose that was proposed for patients in the weight range of 77kg-150kg and evaluated in Phase 3 study (MG0010). For further details on the Applicant's rationale to use the same dose for gMG patients with weight &gt;150 kg as that of gMG patients with weight 77 kg to 150kg and its assessment are presented in Section 8.1.4.
L117: The Applicant conducted a Phase 1 study (Study UP0112 – randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study) evaluating single doses (SD) of 0.05, 0.10, 0.20, and 0.40 mg/kg; and multiple doses (MD) of 0.2 mg/kg for 7 days by subcutaneous administration in adult healthy volunteers. PK and PD (total C5 levels; and complement inhibition [ex vivo %sRBC lysis or hemolysis]) were collected. The Applicant noted that across all dose groups, plasma levels of total C5 increased following a single dose and multiple doses. The mean maximum total C5 levels reached 81µg/mL, 99µg/mL, 99µg/mL, 116 µg/mL postdose following single doses of 0.05, 0.1, 0.20, and 0.40 mg/kg respectively. There were no notable changes observed in C5 concentration across the time points for the placebo groups. For MD cohort, 0.2 mg/kg for 7 days, the mean maximum total C5 levels reached 151µg/mL. The observed increase in C5 is due to the prolongation of the half-life of C5 bound to zilucoplan and not due to increase in available free C5. The Applicant noted that the increases in C5 level coincided with increased plasma level of zilucoplan.
L118: For complement inhibition, the Applicant found that the mean maximum percent inhibition of sRBC lysis compared to baseline reached 52%, 86%, 87%, and 95% postdose following single doses of 0.05, 0.1, 0.20, and 0.40 mg/kg, respectively, suggesting dose-dependent complement inhibition. There was no inhibition of sRBC lysis for the pooled placebo group across all time points. For MD cohort, 0.2 mg/kg for 7 days, the mean percent inhibition of sRBC lysis reached &gt;95% after first dosing and continued through the 7 days of dosing. It was also noted that the maximum %sRBC lysis inhibition i.e., peak PD effect coincided with the time for peak concentration (T_{max}) of zilucoplan for both single doses and multiple dose cohorts. The overall
L119: PK and PD data from this Phase 1 study suggested correlation between PK and complement inhibition.
L120: In the study UP0113 (ethnbridging study) at 0.3 mg/kg dose, after multiple administration (once daily for 14 days), at 24 hours post last dose, the increase in total C5 and the inhibition of %sRBC lysis in white subjects was approximately 64% and 95% respectively at steady state.
L121: PK and PD and safety/tolerability information from the Phase 1 study UP0112 along with clinical experience of zilucoplan in Phase 2 studies for paroxysmal nocturnal hemoglobinuria (PNH) indication led to the selection of two doses, 0.1 mg/kg and 0.3 mg/kg, for Phase 2 study (MG0009) in gMG subjects. The weight-band based dosing was also used in this Phase 2 study (Table 9).
L122: **Table 9. Phase 2 (MG0009) and Phase 3 (MG0010) Zilucoplan Doses by Weight Brackets**
L123: Nominal Target Dose (mg/kg)	Actual Dose (mg)	Weight Range (kg)	Dose Range (mg/kg)

L124: Source: Table 1-3 from Summary of Clinical Efficacy (Module 2.7.3)
L125: The results from this Phase 2 study indicated that greater inhibition of complement pathway occurred with 0.3 mg/kg dose (94.9% inhibition at week 12) when compared with the 0.1 mg/kg dose (81.8% inhibition at week 12) (Figure 1). Further, the Applicant found that the dose of 0.3 mg/kg provided superior efficacy pertaining to the primary (QMg) and key secondary (MG-ADL) endpoints and similar safety profile compared to 0.1 mg/kg (refer to Section 15.1). The dose-response for the clinical outcome measures is consistent with the dose-response for the complement inhibition when comparing 0.1 and 0.3 mg/kg dose groups.
L126: Based on the above results from Phase 2 study, the Applicant selected 0.3 mg/kg dose for Phase 3 study (MG0010) that would provide near complete complement inhibition for therapeutic benefit. The weight-band based dosing implemented in Phase 3 study (Table 9) is the same as the proposed weight-band based dosing regimen (Table 8) except that the inclusion of gMG subjects with weight &gt;150 kg is proposed for the labeling (Refer to Section 8.1.4). In Phase 3 study, zilucoplan resulted in complement inhibition of 97.5% by the end of the first week and sustained throughout the 12-week treatment period. The efficacy and safety results for Phase 3 study are discussed in detail in Section 6.2.2.
L127: The Applicant noted that once daily dosing interval is needed to maintain the exposure for complement inhibition and therapeutic benefit. PK simulations conducted by the Applicant indicated that longer dosing intervals cannot achieve adequate exposures and fail to maintain the depth of complement inhibition needed for maximum therapeutic benefit. The Applicant further noted that once every other day dosing is estimated to reduce the zilucoplan trough concentration
L128: by approximately 50%, resulting in inadequate pharmacodynamic effect. Once daily dosing interval of zilucoplan was studied in both Phase 2 and Phase 3 studies.
L129: In summary, based on the above, for the treatment of adult gMG patients who are anti-AChR-antibody positive, the Applicant proposed 0.3 mg/kg once daily subcutaneous dose with fixed dose for each weight range. The proposed weight-band based dosing regimen is further supported by the population PK analysis and simulation as discussed in Sections 8.1.4 and 14.5. The proposed weight-band based dosing regimen is acceptable. The assessment of the Applicant's proposal of the inclusion of gMG patients with weight greater than 150 kg in the labeling is presented in Section 8.1.4.
L130: ## 6.1.2. Mechanistic Evidence of Support
L131: From the Phase 2 study, MG0009, that evaluated two doses, 0.1 mg/kg and 0.3 mg/kg, there was an increase in mean total C5 levels in zilucoplan dose groups at week 1; whereas there was no change in total C5 levels in the placebo group (Figure 2). Though the mean total C5 levels were similar across zilucoplan dose groups at each time point, both 0.1 mg/kg and 0.3 mg/kg dose groups were significantly separated from placebo group from week 1 onwards (Figure 2; total C5 at week 12 for placebo, 0.1 mg/kg, and 0.3 mg/kg respectively 89.06 µg/mL, 152.73 µg/mL, 142.57 µg/mL). Similarly, in the Phase 3 study (MG0010) that evaluated dose of 0.3 mg/kg, the mean total C5 levels reached to 102.6 µg/mL at week 1 (total C5placebo=90.6 µg/mL) and 138.8 µg/mL at Week 12 (total C5placebo=91.3 µg/mL) and was consistent with that observed in Phase 2 study. No change was observed in the placebo group for the total C5 levels in Phase 3 study. The observed increase in C5 is due to the prolongation of the half-life of C5 bound to zilucoplan and not due to increase in available free C5. From the above PD data, it can be concluded that zilucoplan dose groups trended towards an increase in total C5 levels when compared to placebo. It is also noted that near complete complement inhibition observed with zilucoplan is consistent with that of the approved C5 inhibitors.
L132: Overall, the data on the changes in total C5 levels relevant to the pathophysiology of gMG provide mechanistic evidence for zilucoplan and adds support to the effectiveness findings from Phase 3 study MG0010 for patients with gMG who are AChR-antibody positive.

L137: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended to Demonstrate Efficacy
L138: ## 6.2.1. Results of Pooled Analyses
L139: Not applicable.
L140: ## 6.2.2. Study MG0010
L141: ## 6.2.2.1. Design, Study MG0010
L142: ## Overview and Objective
L143: Study MG0010 was a Phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind, placebo-controlled study in 174 adults with gMG designed to demonstrate the efficacy of once-daily subcutaneous injections of zilucoplan. Subjects were randomly assigned (1:1) to 12 weeks of zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg or placebo after stratification by baseline MG-ADL score (9 or less versus 10 or more), baseline QMG score (17 or less versus 18 or more), and geographical region (North America, Europe, East Asia). Subjects were expected to remain on stable doses of all other medications for MG unless otherwise medically indicated.
L144: The primary efficacy endpoint was to evaluate the clinical efficacy of zilucoplan as assessed by the change from baseline on the MG-ADL score after 12 weeks of treatment with zilucoplan.
L145: MG-ADL is an interviewer-administered patient-reported outcome that assesses eight signs and symptoms of MG. Each item is scored on a discrete 0- to 3-point scale (0=none, 1=mild, 2=moderate, and 3=severe). Individual item scores are added to give a total score that can range from 0 (no impairment) to 24 (severe impairment). The subject must not have received a cholinesterase inhibitor &lt;10 hours prior to the MG-ADL assessment.
L146: Secondary efficacy endpoints:
L147: - Between-groups difference in QMG total score at week 12. QMG is a physician- or trained interviewer-administered PRO with 13 item assessing diplopia, ptosis, facial muscles, swallowing, and arm, head, and leg muscle function. Each item is scored on a discrete 0- to 3-point scale (0=none, 1=mild, 2=moderate, and 3=severe). Individual item scores are added to give a total score that can range from 0 (no impairment) to 39 (severe impairment).
- Between-groups difference in Myasthenia Gravis Composite (MGC) total score at Week 12. MGC is comprised of 10 items originating from other scales (i.e., QMG, MMT, MG-ADL). Each item is scored as an integer from 0 to 5. The total score can range from 0 (none) to 50 (severe).
- Between-groups difference in Myasthenia Gravis Quality of Life 15-item Scale – Revised (MG-QOL15r) total score at Week 12. This is a self-administered PRO designed to assess quality of life in MG. Each item is scored on a 0- to 2-point scale (0=not much at all, 1=somewhat, and 2=very much). The total score is the sum of the individual item scores, and ranges from 0 to 30.
L148: Efficacy and safety assessments in this 3-month study were scheduled at screening (28 days before the baseline visit) then baseline (day 1) then weeks 1 (day 8), 2, 4, 8, 12 (day 84), and safety follow-up (6 to 7 weeks after last visit). Safety assessments included metabolic panel, and gamma-glutamyl transferase (GGT) and amylase and lipase levels, among others (hematology, coagulation, urine analysis and pregnancy test, 12-lead ECG, and physical exam).
L149: *Neisseria meningitidis* vaccination was given as needed through the study per standard of care.
L150: ## 6.2.2.2. Eligibility Criteria, MG0010
L151: The main eligibility criteria were as follows:
L152: - Subjects 18 to &lt;75 years of age
- Symptomatic gMG (MGFA Class II-IV)
- Positive test for AChR antibodies
- MG-ADL total score of 6 or more and a QMG total score of 12 or more (four or more of the QMG test items score at 2 or more)
- No change in baseline steroids or immunosuppressive therapy for at least 30 days prior to baseline
- Vaccination with a quadrivalent meningococcal vaccine and, where available, meningococcal serotype B vaccine at least 14 days prior to the first dose of study drug at the Day 1 visit. A booster vaccination should also be administered as clinically indicated, according to the local standard-of-care for subjects who have been previously vaccinated against *Neisseria meningitidis*
- At the time of QMG (but not MG-ADL) scoring, subjects must have been off acetylcholine inhibitor therapy for at least 10 hours
- Negative serum and urine pregnancy tests within 24 hours of first dose; and use of effective contraception during study and 40 days after discontinuation of drug
L153: ## 6.2.2.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, MG0010
L154: Study MG0010 was a Phase 3, multicenter, randomized, double-blind (DB), placebo-control, parallel group study of subjects with gMG.
L155: Approximately 156 subjects were planned (while 174 were enrolled) to be randomized with a ratio of 1:1 to the drug, or the placebo group, stratified by baseline MG-ADL score (\le 9 versus \ge 10) and region (North America, Europe, and East Asia). Roughly 78 subjects per group were expected to have about 94% power to detect a 2.3-point difference on the primary endpoint with standard deviation of 3.7 points. The source of the effect size appears to be based on the Phase 2 study MG0009. The rates of rescue and dropout were assumed to be up to 10% and 5% respectively.
L156: The primary endpoint for this study was the change from the baseline in the MG-ADL (Myasthenia Gravis-Activities of Daily Living) score after twelve weeks of treatment during the 12-week, double-blind treatment period.
L157: The MG-ADL is an 8-item questionnaire that focuses on relevant symptoms and functional performance of activities of daily living (ADL) in patients with MG. The 8 items of the MG-ADL questionnaire were derived from symptom-based components of the original 13-item QMG scale to assess disability secondary to ocular (2 items), bulbar (3 items), respiratory (1 item), and gross motor or limb (2 items) impairment related to effects from MG. In this functional status instrument, each response is graded 0 (normal) to 3 (most severe). The range of total MG-ADL score is 0 to 24. The recall period for MG-ADL is the preceding 7 days.
L158: The three prespecified Family 1 secondary endpoints for this study were:
L159: - Change from baseline to week 12 in QMG score
- Change from baseline to week 12 in MGC (Myasthenia Gravis Composite) score
- Change from baseline to week 12 in MG-QOL15r (Revised Myasthenia Gravis Quality of Life 15-item scale) Survey
L160: The four prespecified Family 2 secondary endpoints for this study were:
L161: - Proportion of subjects who received rescue therapy over the 12-week treatment period
- Proportion of subjects with MG-ADL score of 0 or 1 at week 12 without rescue therapy
- Proportion of subjects with \ge 3 points reduction in MG-ADL score at week 12 without rescue therapy
- Proportion of subjects with \ge 5 points reduction in QMG score at week 12 without rescue therapy
L162: The QMG scoring system consists of 13 items: ocular (2 items), facial (1 item), bulbar (2 items), gross motor (6 items), axial (1 item), and respiratory (1 item); each graded 0 to 3, with 3 being the most severe. The range of total QMG score is 0 to 39.
L163: The study design (double-blinded, randomized, placebo-controlled, parallel group) and the choice of the primary endpoint definition (change from baseline in MG-ADL score during the last week of the 12-week double-blind treatment period) were considered appropriate.
L164: The primary endpoint was analyzed using a mixed effect repeated measures (MMRM) model that included treatment group, baseline MG-ADL score, baseline QMG score, region (North America, Europe, and East Asia) and interactions terms treatment-by-visit and baseline MG-ADL score-by-visit as fixed effects, using an unstructured correlation matrix; subjects were added as random effects in the model; weeks 1, 2, 4, 8, and 12 were included in the model.
L165: Efficacy analyses were based on the modified intent-to-treat (mITT) population defined as all randomized subjects who received at least one dose of study drug grouped by randomized treatment group (for reporting efficacy data). Subjects were grouped based on the assigned treatment.
L166: Any rescue therapy or MG crisis or death were considered as treatment failure, and any missing data due to these reasons were imputed using baseline or last value available, whichever was worse, i.e., worst observation carry forward (WOCF).
L167: Type 1 error was controlled by hierarchical testing. The primary endpoint was analyzed at the 0.05 level. If the primary endpoint were significant, the three Family 1 secondary endpoints would be analyzed at the 0.05 level in the prespecified sequence: QMG, MGC, MG-QOL15r.
L168: If all endpoints in the Family 1 were significant at 2-sided type 1 error of 5%, Family 2 was tested using a Holm procedure at 2-sided type 1 error. The Holm procedure is a multistep, step-down procedure. The procedure started with the smallest p-value that was compared to 0.0125. If the first test showed statistical significance, the treatment effect for the respective endpoint was considered significant. Then, testing proceeded comparing the next smallest p-value to 0.017. If the second test showed statistical significance, testing proceeded comparing the third smallest p-value to 0.025. If the third test showed statistical significance, testing proceeded comparing the largest p-value to 0.05. The procedure stopped when a step yielded a nonsignificant result, i.e., once an ordered p-value was not significant, the remaining larger p-values were not evaluated.
L169: ## 6.2.2.4. Results of Analyses, Study MG0010
L170: ## 6.2.2.4.1. Subject Disposition, Demographic and Baseline Characteristics
L171: This study enrolled the first subject on September 17, 2019, and last subject completed on December 30, 2021. The study report was created on June 30, 2022.
L172: Of the 239 screened subjects, 63 subjects were screen failures, and two subjects withdrew consents before the randomization. In total, 174 subjects were randomized and treated (placebo: N=88; zilucoplan: N=86).
L173: Overall discontinuation rates were low. Eight subjects (4.6%) did not complete the study. Demographic variables were balanced between treatment groups. Baseline medications for gMG were balanced between treatment groups and included cholinesterase inhibitors (85%), prednisone (41%), and other corticosteroids (22%).

L190: ## 6.2.2.4.2. Efficacy Results of the Primary Endpoint
L191: The results reported by the Applicant were confirmed by the statistical reviewer.
L192: The primary analysis of MG-ADL included 174 subjects. There was a statistically significantly greater mean reduction in the change in MG-ADL total score from baseline to week 12 of the 12-week double-blind treatment phase for zilucoplan compared with placebo. The difference in least squares mean (LSM) (95% CI) was -2.09 (-3.24, -0.95) for zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg versus placebo (p&lt;0.001).

L197: The mixed model repeated measures (MMRM) model included treatment group (drug or placebo), region (North America, Europe, and East Asia), MG-ADL total score at baseline, QMG score at baseline, visit (week 1, 2, 4, 8 and 12), treatment by visit and baseline MG-ADL score by visit interactions. An unstructured covariance structure was used. P-values were for the comparison of treatment groups in change from baseline to week 12.
L198: Any rescue therapy, MG crisis, or death were considered as treatment failure, and any related missing data were imputed using baseline or last value available, whichever was worse, i.e., WOCF.
L199: A total of two subjects died during the double-blind period: one in the zilucoplan group and one in the placebo group.
L200: Both subjects missed the week 12 (day 84) assessment for MG-ADL score since they both withdrew from the investigational medicinal product and died before the week 12 timepoint. One subject (b) (6) discontinued on day 17 and died on day 75; one subject (b) (6) discontinued on day 32 and died on day 43. Both subjects were included in the MMRM model with their day 84 MG-ADL score imputed using WOCF, i.e., the worst of baseline, or last value available, whichever was worse. For example, subject (b) (6) discontinued on day 17 and died on day 75; and, therefore, was missing the week 12 (day 84) assessment for MG-ADL score. This subject was included in the MMRM model with day 84 score set as 11.
L201: The site inspection team noted some protocol deviations including the “acetylcholinesterase inhibitor therapy for less than 10 hours prior to evaluation.” An information request was sent, and the Applicant responded by sending an additional protocol deviation analysis dataset named
L202: “DV10_May” in Study Number (SN) 38 with AVISIT and AVISITN populated allowing to identify the timepoints (baseline, day 8, day 15, day 29, day 57, day 84) in which the deviations occurred. The impact on primary efficacy result was small since these protocol deviations were rescue medication use and considered as treatment failure with a “WOCF” approach. The impact of deviations that happened at baseline before randomization, were minimal because of the randomization. There are 12 subjects (6 in each arm) in the protocol deviation analysis dataset with protocol deviation term (DVTERM) = “INCL6 not met, and subject has a QMG score of &lt;12 at screening or baseline or are on acetylcholinesterase inhibitor therapy for less than 10 hours.” Even when excluding these 12 subjects from the primary analysis, the result is still significant (Diff -1.97, p =0.0012).
L203: ## **Additional Analysis After Refreshed Data Received in SN 38 With More Details About Protocol Deviations**
L204: There are two subjects, one from each arm: [REDACTED](b) (6) from placebo arm and [REDACTED](b) (6) from drug arm, who had received acetylcholinesterase inhibitor therapy &lt;10 hours prior to day 84 assessments (Statistical Analysis System [SAS] dataset “dv10_may” submitted in SN 38, DVTERM= “Any change to dosing with cholinesterase inhibitor less than 10 hours prior to evaluation” and AVISIT= “day 84”). The result of the primary analysis remains significant even with the removal of these two subjects from primary efficacy analysis (mean diff -2.14 with p=0.0003).
L205: There are 10 subjects, 5 from each arm, who had acetylcholinesterase inhibitor therapy &lt;10 hours affecting post baseline (any of the 5 timepoints from day 8 to day 84) assessments. (SAS dataset “dv10_may” submitted in SN 38, DVTERM= “Any change to dosing with cholinesterase inhibitor less than 10 hours prior to evaluation” and AVISIT in (“day 8”, “day 15”, “day 29”, “day 57”, “day 84”). Two subjects in the placebo arm were flagged for more than one visit(s); one subject [REDACTED](b) (6) visited five times and one subject [REDACTED](b) (6) visited two times. In total 10 subjects with 15 lines of post baseline records were affected. By removing these from the main analysis, the result is still significant: mean diff -2.14 with p&lt;0.001. Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) was from site 119 which was one of the sites selected for site inspection.

L226: As can be seen in the table above, the efficacy conclusion holds under sensitivity analyses using CFS by excluding the 16 subjects (9 in the drug arm and 7 in the placebo arm) who missed visits for COVID-19 related reasons such as decision to self-quarantine, diagnosis with COVID-19, and site/authority-imposed COVID-19 precautions.

L247: Since all endpoints in Family 1 were significant, Family 2 was tested. Two of the four secondary endpoints were significant in Family 2: MG-ADL responders (\ge 3-point) and QMG responders (\ge 5-point).
L248: The four prespecified **Family 2** secondary endpoints for this study were:
L249: - Proportion of subjects receiving rescue therapy over the 12-week treatment period
- Proportion of subjects with MG-ADL score of 0 or 1 at week 12 without rescue therapy
- Proportion of subjects with \ge 3 points reduction in MG-ADL score at week 12 without rescue therapy
- Proportion of subjects with \ge 5 points reduction in QMG score at week 12 without rescue therapy
L250: Family 2 was tested using a multistep, step-down Holm procedure where the endpoints were tested in order of smallest p-value to largest p-value: comparing the smallest p to 0.0125, 0.017, 0.025, and 0.05. The nominal p-values for the four secondary endpoint in Family 2 were 0.1003, 0.0885, &lt;0.001, and 0.0012. After the Holm procedure, the third and fourth endpoints in Family 2 were statistically significant. Missing data were imputed as nonresponder.

L271: The Applicant's subgroup analysis simply reported the observed descriptive summary such as mean and SD (standard deviation). Out of the total 6 versions of the study SAP, the original version did state that the same methodology of the primary efficacy analysis would be repeated for pre-defined subgroups, and that the result would be viewed as exploratory. The later five versions of SAP changed this part to reporting descriptive results with no statistical testing, citing their internal SAP template and team decision as the reason. The study report was created using the last version of the SAP. The original SAP regarding subgroup analysis reflects what has been used in recent drug reviews, i.e., applying the same methodology for primary efficacy analysis to pre-defined subgroups.
L272: Therefore, the same MMRM model used in the primary endpoint analysis including the WOCF (worst observation carried forward) approach, was rerun on the subsets, and the estimates were reported descriptively without p-values, as can be seen in the above table.

L287: ## **Statistical Issues and Conclusions**
L288: No major statistical issues were identified. There was only one single Phase 3 study to review, and it provided statistical evidence that zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg is effective in treating patients with gMG.
L289: The efficacy results obtained from the statistical analyses of the one pivotal study support the conclusion that zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg is effective in treating patients with gMG.
L290: Most enrolled subjects completed the study. All completers entered the extension. These factors were balanced.
L291: Subjects were balanced demographically except for sex, which does not appear to have biased the efficacy results.
L292: Subjects resembled the wider MG population (mean age 53 years, 28% &gt;65 years, mean BMI 31 kg/m2, 74% white) and were balanced in these factors between groups. The two groups were geographically balanced (52% were North American, 39% were European, and 9% were Asian.).
L293: Baseline myasthenia characteristics were balanced between groups as reflected in disease duration, disease severity, mean age at disease onset of 44 years, mean disease duration 9 years, median MG-ADL 10, median QMG 18, 100% on concomitant medications used to treat MG (97% on cholinesterase inhibitors, 87% on steroids, 69% on immunoglobulin or plasma exchange), which were balanced between groups.

L301: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L302: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L303: Widespread inflammatory effects and liver and pancreas histopathology, with correlating clinical pathology changes, were consistent findings in the 4-, 13-, and 39-week toxicity studies conducted in cynomolgus monkey.
L304: In a 4-week study in the monkey (0, 1, 2, or 4 mg/kg/day), drug-related effects included elevated bile acids and lipase and peribiliary mixed cell inflammation in the liver at the MD and HD.
L305: In the 13-week study (0, 0.25, 1, 2, or 10 mg/kg/day), hepatic and pancreatic enzymes were increased in individual animals at all doses and bile acids were increased at the HD. Histopathologic changes in the pancreas included marked inflammation (vasculitis), acinar degeneration, and ductular hyperplasia with correlative increases in amylase and lipase in one HD male and less severe pancreatic acinar degeneration in single females from each of the \ge 1 mg/kg/day dose groups. Following a 4-week recovery period, acinar atrophy and ductular hyperplasia with mononuclear infiltrates in the pancreas and bridging bile duct and oval cell hyperplasia with fibrosis in the liver were found in one of the two HD males examined. Multi-organ mononuclear infiltrates in the pancreas, kidney, tongue, esophagus, salivary gland, thyroid, rectum, stomach, and urinary bladder were observed at all doses in males and females. The pancreas findings at the HD were considered dose-limiting.
L306: In the 39-week study (0, 0.25, 1, 2, 4, or 6 mg/kg/day), all doses were associated with inflammatory cell infiltrates of most organs. In 3 animals that were sacrificed early due to moribund condition (LD animal on Day 245 with colitis, MHD (4 mg/kg) and HD animals on days 52 and 203, respectively, with skin ulcerations and erosions) histopathology findings included liver fibrosis (HD) and pancreatic acinar degeneration (all 3 animals) or pancreatic duct degeneration/regeneration with acinar atrophy (LD), with associated increases in amylase and lipase at the 2 highest doses. Pancreatic acinar cell degeneration with fibrosis/fibroplasia was also found in 1 MD (2 mg/kg) animal at the scheduled terminal sacrifice. A NOAEL was not established; at what was considered the highest tolerated dose (2 mg/kg/day), exposure was approximately 2X the maximal predicted clinical exposure.
L307: **Table 27. Estimated Safety Margins.**
L308: Study	NOAEL or highest tolerated dose (mg/kg/day)	Observed exposure at NOAEL or highest tolerated dose	Safety margin modelling approacha	Safety margin based on ethnic bridging studyb

L312: c Safety margins based on highest tolerated dose.
L313: Adverse reproductive and developmental effects of zilucoplan (0, 1, 2, or 4 mg/kg/day) were observed in a male fertility study and an enhanced pre- and postnatal (with embryofetal component) development study in monkey (reference Pregnancy and Lactation section).
L314: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L315: The main safety concern with inhibitors of complement C5 activity is infection with encapsulated organisms such as *Neisseria* species (e.g., particularly *N. meningitidis* for C5 targeting therapy) and, in children, *Streptococcus pneumoniae* and *Hemophilus influenzae* Type
L316: B. Immunosuppressed patients may also be at risk for atypical bacterial or fungal infections (e.g., aspergillosis).
L317: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L318: Not applicable.
L319: ## 7.3.1. Adverse Events Identified in Postmarket Experiences
L320: Not applicable. Zilucoplan is a new molecular entity.
L321: ## 7.3.2. Expectations on Safety
L322: Because zilucoplan is a complement C5 inhibitor, there is a risk for meningococcal infection for which a Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS) will be imposed. Warnings and Precautions in labeling will address meningococcal infections and other serious infections.
L323: Pancreatitis and other serious pancreatic adverse events have been identified in these studies and will also be described in Warnings and Precautions.
L324: Pharmacovigilance at FDA will conduct ongoing safety evaluations of post-market reports of serious infections and pancreatic adverse events.
L325: The safety of zilucoplan in women who are pregnant or breastfeeding, and in infants who are breastfeeding, has not been characterized in these clinical trials. Because zilucoplan will be used in women of childbearing potential, a pregnancy outcomes study and a lactation pharmacokinetic study will be requested post-marketing requirements (see Section 24).
L326: ## 7.3.3. Additional Safety Issues From Other Disciplines
L327: Not applicable.
L328: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L329: Data from placebo-controlled phase and extensions of studies MG0010, MG0009, and MG0011 form the basis for clinical safety evaluation in this review. Pivotal Study MG0010 enrolled 174 subjects. The dose-finding phase 2 study MG0009 (44 subjects randomized to 3 dose levels), which had both a placebo-controlled portion and its own extension, plays a supportive role. The placebo-controlled studies (MG0009 DB and MG0010) and long-term extensions (MG0009 OLE and MG0011) in MG were analyzed separately. The long-term safety data was utilized for the analysis of pancreatic events, which took longer to manifest than the 12-week duration of the placebo-controlled studies.
L330: The Applicant's mapping of verbatim terms to Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) Preferred Terms (PTs) is acceptable. Safety analyses were based on preferred terms (PT). However, in the case of verifying the Applicant's proposed AE table for the prescribing information), pooling of PT terms resulted in slightly different adverse event counts. Treatment-emergent (TE) was not defined in the protocols—see applicable discussions of treatment-emergent adverse events. These studies used the National Cancer Institute Common Terminology Criteria for Adverse Events criteria for assessing adverse event severity. The definitions of SAEs were compliant with 21 CFR.
L331: Because of issues with the quality of the AE and lab records data in studies MG0009 and MG0010, the safety datasets for studies MG0009, MG0010, and MG0011 were analyzed separately. The Applicant's pooled analyses were not utilized in this review, as the pooling scheme was inadequate
L332: (b)(4)
L333: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L334: In the original submission (excluding the 120-day update data), the Applicant reported 213 subjects with gMG were exposed to the to-be-marketed investigational medicinal product dose of 0.3 mg/kg, 87 of whom received the 0.3 mg/kg dose for at least 1 year.
L335: There were approximately 260 patient-years of exposure to any zilucoplan dose and approximately 254 patient-years of exposure to zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg (to-be-marketed dose) excluding the 120-day update. At the 120-day update there were 329 patient-years of exposure to zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg – 191 subjects for at least 6 months and 131 subjects for at least 1 year [ref: Table 1-4 in Applicant's 0001 submission, Summary of Clinical Safety 120-day safety update]. The Applicant's exposure numbers could not be exactly replicated (despite multiple IRs), although the estimates were within a few patient-years of the Applicant's calculation.

L388: ## 7.6.1.4. Adverse Events Leading to Treatment Discontinuation
L389: Treatment discontinuation is not a meaningful measure of the clinical significance of an AE or SAE in these studies. There were no dose modification/discontinuation criteria in the protocols, and therefore, dose modification was at the discretion of the investigator (or sponsor). Few investigators attributed pancreatic SAEs as related to zilucoplan. Therefore, zilucoplan was usually continued through acute pancreatic conditions, including SAEs.
L390: Nonetheless, based on terms used in the ADAE datasets, the following list shows the distribution of “withdrawals” and “interruptions”, terms not further defined:
L391: - Study MG0009: 22 in 14 subjects. Some subjects had both SAEs and non-SAEs.Withdrawals: Six subjects (one on placebo, five on zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg)Two deaths, both in OL portion: one pancreatic cancer death and one COVID death
- One subject receiving placebo in the double-blind period for MG exacerbation (SAE)
- Three in OL portion: one SAE of Grade 3 pneumonia related to zilucoplan, one AE of moderate dizziness, and one AE of mild musculoskeletal pain
- Interruptions: 16 in 8 subjects (1 on placebo, 1 on zilucoplan 0.1 mg/kg, and 6 on zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg)SAEs—myocardial infarction, noncardiac chest pain, syncope; sepsis; nephrolithiasis, abdominal pain (on placebo), diverticulitis, systemic inflammatory response syndrome.
- Non-SAEs—Two high lipase; dehydration, hypokalemia (on placebo); viral infection, abdominal abscess, rectal fistula (on placebo); atrial fibrillation
- Study MG0010: 23 in 13 subjects. The hepatic enzyme increase occurred after 2 weeks of zilucoplan (GGT &gt;10X upper limit of normal [ULN]) and is discussed in a later section

L406: There was a discrepancy in AE counts between the reviewer's analysis and the Applicant's analysis in the high-lipase AE counts. The initial count was 7 in the zilucoplan arm and 1 in the placebo arm. However, subsequent inspection of the data in ADAE (study MG0010) revealed that two of the high-lipase AEs (one in each arm) were not actually treatment emergent, as they were listed as having onset on the same exact day therapy was initiated in MG0010, and therefore would give a misleading impression of having been associated with the therapy in MG0010. The same issue occurred with the high-amylase AEs, where a high-amylase AE in the placebo arm occurred on the same day of treatment initiation in MG0010.
L407: In the double-blind period of Study MG0009, there were 169 AEs in 41 (93%) subjects, evenly divided among the 3 treatment arms. The most common AE was headache with 15 episodes (13 subjects) evenly divided among the 3 arms; 4 of these AEs were 'migraine with aura' in one subject over 4 days and were in the 0.3 mg/kg arm. The next most common AEs were each divided evenly among arms—11 injection site reactions (9 subjects, more common in zilucoplan group), 5 MG exacerbations (five subjects), and five AEs of dizziness (five subjects).
L408: The higher rate of the AE of lipase increased in the zilucoplan group than the placebo group is consistent with the nonclinical pancreatic findings.
L409: In study MG0009 OLE portion, there were 447 AEs in 42 subjects, which were balanced between the 0.1 mg/kg group (225 AEs in 22 subjects) and the 0.3 mg/kg group (222 AEs in 20 subjects). The most common AEs representing the most subjects were balanced between groups, and included upper respiratory tract symptoms, cough, gastrointestinal (GI) symptoms, injection site reactions, and myalgia/arthralgia.
L410: In Study MG0011, there were 1,374 AEs in 164 subjects. The most frequent AE was headache (33 subjects), followed by MG exacerbation (28), COVID-19 (25), nonspecific viral symptoms. Injection site reactions, and dizziness, among others.
L411: See Section 7.7.1 for further discussion of treatment emergent adverse events related to pancreatitis and other pancreatic conditions.
L412: ## 7.6.1.6. Laboratory Findings
L413: This section focuses on lipase levels, which were a frequently scheduled laboratory assessment in these protocols. Serum lipase elevation is the result of pancreatic acinar cell injury and is a sensitive and specific indicator of exocrine pancreatic acinar cell injury.
L414: ## Serum Lipase Levels in the Laboratory Data
L415: In these studies, serum lipase (and amylase) elevations were mostly not captured as AEs but rather as scheduled laboratory data throughout these studies. Conversely, lipase elevations that occurred in most pancreatic AEs were not captured in these laboratory data. Therefore, the data reviewed here, which are based on the ADBL dataset, are for scheduled lipase levels unless otherwise noted. In ADBL there were relatively few unscheduled serum lipase levels.
L416: An extensive analysis of the serum lipase data can be seen in Section 17.2 of this review.
L417: In the placebo-controlled studies, mean serum lipase level increased by 40-44 U/L by day 57 in the zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg group compared to placebo (2 U/L increase), and remained elevated by 20 U/L over baseline at day 84 (end of study in MG0010). This same trend also occurred in subjects newly initiating zilucoplan after being on placebo in antecedent studies MG0009 or MG0010.
L418: In Study MG0010, six (7%) subjects on zilucoplan had an increase from a normal baseline lipase to a level &gt;3x ULN by day 57 or 84, as compared to 0 (0%) on placebo (p=0.0133, 2-sided exact trend test). Lipase (or amylase) &gt;3x ULN is one of the three American College of Gastroenterology criteria3 for acute pancreatitis (Tenner et al. 2013) (the other two criteria are typical abdominal pain and characteristic findings on abdominal imaging).
L419: There were only a few cases of isolated amylase elevation, and most lipase elevations occurred in conjunction with amylase elevations. See Section 17. The amylase analysis does not change my conclusions about lipase.
L420: ## 7.6.1.7. Assessment of Drug-Induced Liver Injury, All Studies
L421: There were no serious drug-related hepatic disorders.
L422: In Study MG0010, there were two subjects with liver enzymes greater than 10X ULN, which involved only GGT:
L423: - Subject (b) (6) in the placebo group in Japan with GGT=650 U/L (ULN=61 U/L) on day 84 (final visit) of MG0010. This subject was rolled into MG0011. At a rescue therapy visit 9 months (Day 267) into MG0011, GGT=703 U/L (ULN=61 U/L). In MG0011, this subject had 6 grade-1 AEs on MG0011 Days 33 through 421, in the following chronologic order—
L424: 3 American College of Gastroenterology (ACG) criteria for acute pancreatitis: “The diagnosis of AP is most often established by the presence of two of the three following criteria: (i) abdominal pain consistent with the disease, (ii) serum amylase and/or lipase greater than three times the upper limit of normal, and/or (iii) characteristic findings from abdominal imaging.”
L425: headache, hyperlipidemia, injection site bruise, nausea, oropharyngeal pain, and productive cough.

- Subject (b) (6) in 0.3 mg/kg group in the United States with GGT=582 U/L (ULN=50 U/L) on day 29 (early termination), and GGT=640 U/L a month later (safety follow-up). This was classified as a Grade-1 AE in the database. This subject's liver function was normal at screening but not baseline (GGT=286 U/L), and there was no history of liver disease. This is unexpected and suggests an error. On day 29 of zilucoplan, this subject had zilucoplan withdrawn due to increased liver enzymes. See Figure 3 (ref: Applicant's narratives document).
