L444: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L445: ## 7.7.1. Pancreatitis and Associated Pancreatic Conditions
L446: ## Issue
L447: A pancreatic safety signal was identified in the human studies, which is consistent with the nonclinical findings. There was a high incidence of pancreatic events in the 254 patient-years (330 patient-years in the 120-day update) of exposure to zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg in these data. There were also significantly higher rates of high-lipase AEs and serum lipase elevations in the zilucoplan groups (mostly 0.3 mg/kg) compared to placebo. Pancreatic AEs did not occur during the 12-week placebo-controlled studies but only in the long-term extensions, where median time
L448: to onset was 180 days. Because of this delayed effect, proportions of subjects experiencing pancreatic AEs (PT terms including pancreatitis, pancreatic cyst/pseudocyst, and infected pancreatic cyst/pseudocyst) could not be assessed by comparison to placebo. However, the incidence of acute pancreatitis in this program was higher than expected for the general U.S. population.
L449: ## **Background**
L450: The label should inform patients and providers that pancreatic conditions have occurred with the use of zilucoplan in these studies.
L451: Nonclinical findings included elevated lipase (and amylase) and pancreatic acinar cell degeneration and inflammation (histologic focal pancreatitis). In the human studies, lipase and amylase were monitored with scheduled laboratory testing at all visits, but baseline pancreatic ultrasound was not required. Most investigators did not consider pancreatic events related to zilucoplan, and therefore did not discontinue zilucoplan when a pancreatic SAE occurred. However, there were no explicit dose-modification criteria for pancreatic or other events in these protocols. Drug discontinuation was variable. There were two deaths from pancreatic adenocarcinoma, during which zilucoplan was continued. The Applicant presented an argument that pancreatic cancer was not from zilucoplan, as it often onsets decades before presentation. However, this does not exclude a possible contributory role of zilucoplan.
L452: ## **Assessment**
L453: **Pancreatic Adverse Events:** AEs and SAEs with the terms “pancreas” or “pancreatic” in their MedDRA description.
L454: Classic risk factors for pancreatitis are prior pancreatitis (probably the strongest risk factor) as well as gallbladder disease, excessive alcohol intake, cigarette smoking, obesity, diabetes, family history of pancreatitis, and various drugs, among others such as metabolic disease with which many of these conditions are already associated (Zhong et al. 2023). A history of any of these risk factors, none of which is as strong a predictor as prior pancreatic events, does not exclude a contributory role for zilucoplan.
L455: In the double-blind period of Study MG0009, there were no pancreatic AEs. In Study MG0010, there were no pancreatic AEs. Pancreatic AEs did not occur in the 3-month placebo-controlled periods. In the long-term extensions MG0009 and MG0011, seven subjects experienced at least one pancreatic adverse event and all subjects were on zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg at the time of the event.
L456: **Table 32** summarizes cases of pancreatitis, pancreatic cysts, and pancreatic cancer in these studies. All occurred on a dose of 0.3 mg/kg and in the long-term extensions. This table was constructed by piecing together the narratives, ADAE and ADMH datasets, and the individual clinical study reports.

L463: In Table 32 there were four subjects ( [REDACTED](b)(6) ) with pancreatitis, excluding subject [REDACTED](b)(6) who had “marked acute inflammation” on a fine-needle aspirate of a pancreatic mass. Only one of these subjects ( [REDACTED](b)(6) ), who had a worsened pancreatic cyst SAE, had a history of a pancreatic condition before starting zilucoplan. For subject [REDACTED](b)(6), focal chronic pancreatitis was found on histopathology of the cyst, so this case is counted as a case of pancreatitis though subclinical prior to biopsy.
L464: For subject [REDACTED](b) (6), further information was requested from the Applicant for reports, pathology results, and procedure notes. Based on the information submitted, it appears that subject [REDACTED](b) (6) with no history of a pancreatic condition before starting zilucoplan, developed fatal advanced pancreatic adenocarcinoma during Study MG0011 while on zilucoplan. Reportedly, no biopsy could be obtained, as the pancreatic tumor was inoperable. Zilucoplan was continued through the 2-month workup until the final diagnosis and treatment. The histology reports the Applicant submitted were for two fine-needle aspirates of the mass, which showed marked acute inflammation and atypical ductal cells. However, clinically this subject was managed as advanced pancreatic adenocarcinoma. The subject had an extremely elevated pancreatic cancer marker (CA-19) and unexplained ascites. The pancreatic tumor was considered inoperable, as it invaded celiac axis and major portal vessels. Zilucoplan was eventually discontinued, and chemotherapy for pancreatic adenocarcinoma was initiated. The subject was placed in hospice care until death seven weeks later.
L465: There were five pancreatic cyst AEs in three subjects, and there are multiple instances in the table above for the same subject having both a pancreatic cyst and pancreatitis. One of the subjects with a pancreatic cyst had a prior history of pancreatitis before starting zilucoplan. Based on the data in Table 32, the median time to first pancreatic event was 180 days. There were no pancreatic AEs in the 12-week placebo-controlled periods to allow comparison to placebo.
L466: According to the ADMH (medical history) datasets for studies MG0009 and MG0010, six subjects had a prior history of a pancreatic condition at program enrollment before starting zilucoplan:

L471: As expected, a higher proportion of subjects (2 of 6) with a history of a pancreatic condition had a pancreatic-related AE in MG0011. For the six subjects with a history of a pancreatic condition prior to entering these studies ("History(+)"), two subjects had at least one pancreatic AE ("AE(+)") and the remaining four subjects did not ("AE(-)"). Among the 206 subjects without a pancreatic history ("History(-)"), five subjects had at least one pancreatic AE ("AE(+)") as compared to 201 subjects who did not ("AE(-)"). Therefore, 5 (2.4%) of 206 subjects without a pancreatic history experienced at least one pancreatic AE in these studies, as compared to 2 (33%) of six subjects with a pancreatic history prior to zilucoplan.
L472: These data are consistent with the known, higher risk of a future exocrine pancreatic condition given a history of same. It does not exclude a contributory or even causative role of zilucoplan in elevating this risk. Most subjects (5 of 7 as shown in column "AE(+)") with a pancreatic AE did not have a history of a pancreatic condition before starting zilucoplan.
L473: In these studies, infection of a pancreatic cyst or pseudocysts occurred as a postoperative complication of endoscopic intervention for a newly identified or worsening pancreatic cyst or pseudocyst.
L474: In the 120-day follow-up data for MG0011, another subject without a pancreatic history had a nonserious mild AE of "pancreatic steatosis" (preferred term), which is a relatively new diagnostic term for fatty infiltration of the pancreas (believed to be similar in etiology to the fatty liver associated with metabolic disease).
L475: ## Adverse Events of Lipase Elevations
L476: Lipase elevations were seen in the nonclinical studies.
L477: Lipase is produced in the pancreas and released into the circulation from pancreatic acinar cell injury. Low-normal levels of serum lipase are not of clinical concern here, and lower limit of normal (LLN) for lipase was 0 U/L in these data at some laboratories used in Study MG0010. Although serum amylase elevation also usually occurs in pancreatitis, amylase is found in other organ systems and has a much shorter serum half-life than lipase, so it is not as sensitive or specific for pancreatitis as lipase.
L478: Therefore, this section focuses on lipase elevations, most of which were also associated with amylase elevations.
L479: In the double-blind period for Study MG0009, the following three subjects had AEs (non-SAEs) of high serum lipase:
L480: - Placebo arm in the double-blind period:MG0009-(b)(6): High lipase on Days 8 and 29. Medical history (MH) of hypertension and coronary artery disease.
- MG0009-(b)(6): High lipase on Day 29. Medical history of chronic pancreatitis, obesity, and metabolic disease. Dose interrupted.
- Zilucoplan 0.1 mg/kg arm in the double-blind period: None
- Zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg arm the double-blind period:
L481: – MG0009-(b)(6): High lipase and amylase on Day 57. This subject was one of the two fatal pancreatic carcinoma cases.
L482: In Study MG0009 OLE portion, the following two subjects had AEs (no SAEs) of high serum lipase (Days since first dose in MG0009 DB portion), and dose was not interrupted in either case:
L483: - MG0009-(b)(6): High lipase on Day 140 on 0.1 mg/kg zilucoplan. Medical history noncontributory.
- MG0009-(b)(6): High lipase on Day 168 on 0.1 mg/kg zilucoplan. Also had high lipase on Day 29 of study MG0009 (see above). Medical history of chronic pancreatitis, obesity, and metabolic disease
L484: In study MG0010, the reviewer's analysis was consistent with the Applicant's counts in Table 2 in the label and showed that 1 (1.2%) of 88 subjects on placebo and 7 (8%) of 86 subjects on zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg had a high-lipase AE.
L485: In study MG0011, eight subjects had nine high-lipase AEs (no SAEs), and all were on zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg. One AE was Grade 3 (severe), three were Grade 2 (moderate), and five were Grade 1 (mild). Subject (b)(6) had two high-lipase AEs, the first of which (Day 57) resulted in interruption of zilucoplan and the last of which (Day 139) resulted in permanent discontinuation of zilucoplan.
L486: For high-lipase AEs, of which there were 15 such AEs in this program (3 among the 6 subjects with pancreatic history, and 12 among 206 subjects without pancreatic history), it is not surprising that a pancreatic history was associated with a higher risk of high-lipase AEs on zilucoplan, although the counts are relatively small.
L487: In the 120-day follow-up data, 9 additional subjects, none of whom had a pancreatic history, had AEs (included one SAE) of lipase elevation, and one subject had an AE of 'pancreatic steatosis' (Paul and Shihaz 2020).4
L488: **Adverse Events of Amylase Elevation.** There were adverse events of elevated amylase in study MG0010, with 5 cases in the zilucoplan arm and 1 case in the placebo arm. There was a single, isolated high-amylase AE (not associated with an AE of high lipase) in study MG0010, and a single isolated high-amylase AE in MG0011. This examination of amylase AEs did not change the lipase assessments above.
L489: ## **Summary of Pancreatic and High-Lipase Adverse Events in the Three Studies**
L490: The human data suggest that zilucoplan is associated with pathologic effects on the exocrine pancreas, as do the nonclinical data. In humans, these effects include pancreatitis, pancreatic cyst/pseudocyst and/or infected pancreatic cyst/pseudocyst, and high-lipase AEs (including those &gt;3xULN in the American College of Gastroenterology criteria for acute pancreatitis). Acinar cell damage leads to leakage of pancreatic enzymes such as lipase (and amylase) into the circulation
L491: 4 Pancreatic steatosis is a relatively newly recognized diagnosis involving fatty infiltration of the pancreas, and is associated with pancreatic scarring, pancreatitis, and possibly pancreatic cancer. The diagnosis of pancreatic steatosis is usually made via U/S, CT, or MRI scan
L492: and are generally believed to lead to pancreatic cyst formation, pancreatitis, and may eventually lead to pancreatic adenocarcinoma.
L493: In the human data, zilucoplan was associated with a higher risk of pancreas-related events in patients with a prior history of pancreatic conditions before starting zilucoplan. Two of 6 subjects with a prior pancreatic history as compared to 5 of 206 subjects without a history suffered a pancreatic event (pancreatitis or cyst) while on zilucoplan. Although a prior pancreatic history increases the risk of future events, this does not exclude a contributory role of zilucoplan.
L494: Discontinuation of zilucoplan in pancreatic SAEs was variable. A few investigators considered pancreatic and lipase AEs to be 'related' to zilucoplan, but most did not. Consequently, zilucoplan was not interrupted during most pancreatic adverse events, including the two deaths from pancreatic adenocarcinoma.
L495: Pancreatitis and pancreatic cysts (newly identified or increased in size while on zilucoplan) did occur in 6 (3%) of 213 subjects, which is high for a program this size (330 patient-years including 120-day update) in myasthenia gravis, a disease not classically associated with pancreatitis or pancreatic cysts. Baseline pancreatic ultrasound was not a scheduled procedure but could have been very useful in assessing the rate of new pancreatic cyst formation and changes in prior cysts. For acute pancreatitis cases only (n=3 which excludes subject (b) (6) with histologic chronic pancreatitis) during the 330 patient-years of the data submitted for MG, the incidence translates to 900 cases per 100,000 patient-years. This rate is considerably higher than the incidence of acute pancreatitis in the general population of the United States, which is 5 to 35 cases per 100,000 person-years (Vege 2023).
L496: Of the six subjects with a prior medical history of pancreatitis or pancreatic cyst before starting zilucoplan, two (33%) had an episode of pancreatitis during these studies. Two subjects died of pancreatic adenocarcinoma within two months of diagnosis, one with and one without a pre-zilucoplan history of a pancreatic condition. Given the rarity of acute pancreatitis in the general population (5 - 35 in 100,000 patient-years), the probability of having three subjects develop acute pancreatitis in this small MG population of about 254 patient-years (330 including the 120-day data) is high.
L497: In most cases, zilucoplan was not interrupted for high-lipase or the occurrence of pancreatic events in these studies. In one case where pancreatitis was not diagnosed despite months of abdominal symptoms, foci of focal chronic pancreatitis were discovered on histology, and this was not listed as an AE of pancreatitis. Additionally, in only a few cases did investigators consider pancreatic-related issues to be "related" to zilucoplan, and even in those few cases, zilucoplan was not necessarily discontinued.
L498: Consistent with the pancreatic adverse events in the extension studies, a significantly higher rate of high-lipase AEs and laboratory elevations of serum lipase occurred in the zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg group compared to placebo. Additionally, mean serum lipase level at scheduled visits increased by 44 U/L, and there were more cases of lipase values &gt;3xULN in the zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg group than the placebo group (no change in mean lipase) beginning at day 57 and beyond.
L499: It is possible that zilucoplan has a complement-mediated adverse effect on the exocrine pancreas not shared by the antibody C5 inhibitors, and it may be related to effect on pancreatic
L500: complement C3 activity. There is also a possibility, but not proven, that the mechanism of action of zilucoplan unlike the biologic C5 inhibitors may involve complement-mediated pancreatic acinar cell injury. Complement C5 appears to exert an anti-inflammatory effect on the pancreas in animals and humans (Bhatia et al. 2001). On the other hand, C3a (part of the C5 cleavage complex) is inflammatory to the pancreas, as it is associated in humans with the severity of subsequent acute pancreatitis following first onset of symptoms a week earlier (Gloor et al. 2003). The effect of C5 inhibition by zilucoplan on levels of C3 and C3a (upstream), C5 and C5b levels in the pancreas, and on potential pancreatic inflammatory effects does not seem to have been investigated in this program or elsewhere. Complement levels and activity of C5, C5a, C5b, C3, C3a, and others and their effects on the pancreas were not assessed in this program.
L501: The product labeling will describe the risk of pancreatitis and other pancreatic events that were seen in these studies. For inclusion in Section 5 (Warnings and Precautions), there needs to be “reasonable evidence of a causal association, but a causal relationship need not to have been definitively excluded” (Guidance for Industry: Warnings and Precautions, Contraindications, and Boxed Warning Sections of Labeling for Human Prescription Drug and Biological Products – Content and Format). Although pancreatic events were not observed during the three-month duration of the placebo-controlled studies, a causal relationship cannot be definitively excluded based on the nonclinical findings and the high incidence of pancreatic events observed in the long-term data in this program.
L502: ## Conclusion
L503: Pharmacovigilance. The Applicant will be requested to perform postmarketing surveillance for serious events related to pancreatitis and other pancreatic adverse events.
L504: Labeling. Pancreatitis and other pancreatic conditions as well as lipase and amylase elevations will be added to the Warnings and Precautions section of the label. The following considerations must be conveyed to patients and providers in the labeling before using zilucoplan:
L505: - Patients and providers should be made aware of the risk of pancreatic events while on zilucoplan. A warning in Section 5 and further description in section 6 will be included. Patients and providers should have a lower threshold for stopping zilucoplan and pursuing a workup for pancreatic disease, if symptoms occur that are suggestive of pancreatitis (e.g., nausea, vomiting, abdominal pain, left shoulder pain, back pain, epigastric pain, and/or weight loss, among others).
- If acute (or exacerbations of chronic) pancreatitis or pancreatic cyst develop while on zilucoplan, the provider and patient should consider whether the benefit outweighs the risk of continuing zilucoplan.
L506: ## 7.7.2. Meningococcal Infection
L507: ## Issue
L508: Susceptibility to infection with encapsulated organisms such as *Neisseria meningitidis* secondary to C5 inhibition.
L509: ## **Background**
L510: C5 inhibitors increase the risk of infection with encapsulated organisms such as *N. meningitidis*. For the other approved C5 inhibitors, there is a boxed warning and REMS for serious meningococcal infections, which may be fatal especially if not treated early. Therefore, meningococcal vaccination and antibiotic prophylaxis are key considerations for starting patients on these drugs.
L511: ## **Assessment**
L512: No cases of infection with *N. meningitidis* or *N. gonorrhoeae* were reported in these data.
L513: ## **Conclusion**
L514: This issue will be addressed in labeling and with a REMS, to harmonize with the other C5 inhibitors.
L515: We note that patients receiving complement inhibitors remain at risk for meningococcal disease even after meningococcal vaccinations (Mbaeyi et al. 2020). In addition, as noted in the consultative review provided by the Division of Vaccines and Related Product Applications/Office of Vaccines Research and Review/CBER (CBER/OVRR/DVPRA/CRB3) dated August 9, 2023, the role of antimicrobial prophylaxis is poorly defined in this patient population without clear recommendations regarding the choice of antimicrobials, timing of prophylaxis relative to vaccination or duration of therapy. The benefit-risk profile of antimicrobial prophylaxis in individuals at high risk for meningococcal infection is also poorly defined, as antimicrobial prophylaxis may not prevent all cases of meningococcal disease and may be associated with an increased risk of antimicrobial resistance. In the absence of appropriately designed clinical studies, the role and best practices for antimicrobial prophylaxis in individuals receiving complement therapy with or without meningococcal vaccination is unknown. The labeling regarding vaccination and antibiotic prophylaxis is in agreement with recommendations provided by CBER/OVRR/DVPRA/CRB3 and by the Division of Anti-Infective (DAI) in a consult dated August 18, 2023. The Applicant will be requested to perform pharmacovigilance for infection, including meningococcal infection and opportunistic infection, and to submit quarterly analyses.
L516: ## 7.7.3. Other Serious Infections
L517: Given the mechanism of action, there may be a theoretical risk of other serious infections associated with zilucoplan.
L518: One subject on zilucoplan 0.3 mg/kg developed Candida endocarditis on day 353, preceded nine months earlier (on Day 71) by bacteremia (*Acinetobacter*, *Brevibacterium*, and *Corynebacterium*), and followed 5 months later (day 503) by staphylococcal bacteremia. This subject was a 71-year-old male with a very complex medical history and very high risk of immunosuppression, including insulin-dependent diabetes mellitus and long-term corticosteroids and antibiotics before and during zilucoplan administration.
L519: A few pancreatic infections were reported, which involved pancreatic cysts/pseudocysts, liver, and/or the peripancreatic or perihepatic spaces, although not from unusual organisms or under unexpected circumstances. Urinary tract infection occurred more often in the zilucoplan than
L520: placebo group in study MG0010, which was not included in the proposed USPI, but at low rates (8% versus 5%). No other unexpected, serious infections occurred in subjects on zilucoplan.
L521: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L522: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L523: ## 8.1.1. Renal Impairment
L524: A renal impairment study (UP0114) assessed the pharmacokinetics of zilucoplan and its metabolites (RA103488 and RA102758) after administration of a single subcutaneous dose of 0.3 mg/kg (weight-band based dosing) in subjects with severe renal impairment (estimated creatinine clearance &lt;30mL/min based on Cockcroft-Gault formula) and matched healthy subjects with normal renal function. Zilucoplan maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) and area under the concentration-time curve (AUC_{0-inf}) was 8% and 13% lower in subjects with severe renal impairment subjects respectively as compared to subjects with normal renal function. Whereas, for metabolite RA102758, C_{max} and AUC_{0-inf} was 12% and 10% lower in subjects with severe renal impairment respectively as compared to subjects with normal renal function. For RA103488, another metabolite, AUC_{0-inf} and C_{max} was 1.4- to 1.5-fold higher in subjects with severe renal impairment respectively as compared to subjects with normal renal function.
L525: For zilucoplan and RA102758, the PK is generally similar across two populations. Based on study UP0113 (zilucoplan dose of 0.3 mg/kg once daily for 14 days), the exposure of RA102758 and RA103488 is approximately 10% of zilucoplan exposure. Due to much lower exposure of RA103488 compared to zilucoplan, the Applicant concludes that the clinical impact of the observed increase in exposure for RA103488 is expected to be negligible. It is also noted that the increase in total C5 concentration at day 3 and day 6 was similar between subjects with normal renal function and subjects with severe renal impairment. Based on urine analysis in the study, zilucoplan and both metabolites are minimally renally excreted in both the normal and severe renal impairment subjects (&lt;1% of dose in total across all analytes).
L526: Overall, the review team agrees with the Applicant's conclusion that severe renal impairment did not influence PK and PD of zilucoplan. The review team agrees with the proposed labeling that no dose adjustment is required in patients with renal impairment. For further details, refer to Section 14.2.3.
L527: ## 8.1.2. Hepatic Impairment
L528: A hepatic impairment study (UP0094) assessed the pharmacokinetics of zilucoplan and its metabolites (RA103488 and RA102758) after administration of a single subcutaneous dose of 0.3 mg/kg (weight-band based dosing) in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh category B with a score of 7 to 9) and matched healthy subjects with normal hepatic function.
L529: Zilucoplan peak exposure C_{max} was similar in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment and normal hepatic function (geometric LSM ratio [90% CI] - 0.95 [0.82, 1.10]). However, the
L530: systemic exposure based on AUC_{0-last} and AUC_{0-inf} was 24% lower in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment compared with subjects with normal hepatic function (geometric LSM ratio [90% CI] - 0.76 [0.65, 0.88]). The peak exposure C_{max} for RA102758 and RA103488 increased by 21% and 16% respectively in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment compared with subjects with normal hepatic function. Whereas the systemic exposure AUC_{0-last} for RA102758 and RA103488 increased by 9% and 22% respectively in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment compared with subjects with normal hepatic function. There was no meaningful difference identified with respect to total C5 levels and complement inhibition (sRBC lysis) between moderate hepatic impairment subjects and normal subjects despite a 24% lower zilucoplan systemic exposure in moderate hepatic impairment subjects.
L531: Overall, the observed decrease in systemic exposure of zilucoplan in moderate hepatic impairment subjects is not expected to be clinically significant. The review team therefore agrees with the proposed labeling that no dose adjustment is required in patients with mild and moderate hepatic impairment. The Applicant will be recommended to include the following in section 12.3 of labeling “The pharmacokinetics of zilucoplan in patients with severe hepatic impairment have not been studied.” For further details, refer to Section 14.2.4.
L532: ## 8.1.3. Ethno-Bridging Study
L533: An ethnobridging study (UP0113) assessed the PK and PD of single and multiple doses of zilucoplan in healthy Japanese and white subjects. The Applicant noted that the peak exposure, C_{max}, and the systemic exposure (AUC) of zilucoplan and metabolites (RA102758 and RA103488) were generally similar in Japanese and white subjects following a single (0.1 mg/kg or 0.3m/kg) and multiple (0.3 mg/kg for 14 days) doses of zilucoplan. The Applicant also noted that the extent and duration of PD response (total C5 levels and %sRBC Lysis Inhibition) appeared to be similar between Japanese and white subjects. The Applicant therefore concludes that zilucoplan dose adjustment for Japanese and white subjects is not warranted. It is noted that any labeling claim based on the above ethnicity assessment is not made by the Applicant. For further details, refer to Section 14.2.2.
L534: ## 8.1.4. Body Weight
L535: A body weight-band based dosing was followed throughout the clinical development program of zilucoplan. The proposed weight-band dosing regimen (Table 8) is consistent with the weight-band dosing regimen evaluated in Phase 3 (MG0010) study (Table 9). Based on population PK analysis, it was confirmed that body weight is a significant covariate, and the simulation further supports the body weight-band based dosing regimen (Section 14.5). Further, the review team evaluated the need for removing the cap for the upper body weight limit proposed in the initial labeling submission (i.e., dosing for patients weighing higher than 150 kg) based on the response to FDA labeling edits (submitted by email dated August 14, 2023). The proposed fixed dose for patients with weight 150 kg and above is 32.4 mg same as the fixed dose for patients with weight ranging from 77 kg to &lt;150 kg.
L536: The simulation of steady state exposures for patients records in the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES) database were conducted by the Applicant utilizing the validated population PK model and proposed dosing regimen as function of body weight. The
L537: Applicant concluded that simulated exposures are comparable across weight ranges of 25 kg to 150 kg for all 3 dosing categories (Figure 16 of Section 14.5). Despite the variability in PK associated with body weight, there was comparable efficacy outcome (mean change from baseline for MG-ADL score) across the evaluated body weight categories (Figure 21 of Section 14.5) in Phase 3 study (MG0010) providing further support for the use of body weight-band based dosing regimen.
L538: Further, the Applicant referred to 5 subjects with weight greater than 150 kg from the Phase 2 and open-label (OL) extension study who were on the fixed dose of 32.4mg, which was the same dose for patients with weight 77 kg to 150 kg. From these 5 subjects, 2 subjects were from Phase 2 study (MG0009); and 3 subjects were from OL extension study (MG0011; who switched from placebo in MG0010 study to 0.3 mg/kg dose (fixed dose of 32.4mg) in MG0011 study) (Table 34). Based on these data and simulations of PK exposures for subjects above 150, the review team concluded that steady state exposures for zilucoplan were comparable for subjects weighing &gt; 77 kg with the 32.4 mg dose Figure 4. In addition, the observed sRBC lysis inhibition was &gt;95% in subjects with body weight 77 to 171 kg with the 32.4 mg dose which suggests comparable PD response across the different body weights. Based on the above, the review team therefore agrees with the body weight-band based dosing regimen presented in Table 8.

L550: ## 8.1.5. Other Intrinsic Factors
L551: The population PK analyses indicated that zilucoplan PK was not affected by age, sex, or race (Section 14.5). Therefore, no dose adjustment is warranted based on these factors.
L552: The Applicant also evaluated the relative bioavailability of zilucoplan at 0.3 mg/kg single dose following subcutaneous administration at different injection sites (abdomen [reference site], thigh, and upper arm) in healthy subjects. There were no notable differences in peak (C_{max}) and systemic (AUC_{0-t}) exposure of zilucoplan between abdomen and arm injection sites or between abdomen and thigh injection sites. For both C_{max} and AUC_{0-t}, the geometric least squares mean ratio for the comparison of arm versus abdomen and thigh versus abdomen was close to 1 with 90% CI within criteria of 80% to 125%. The review team therefore agrees with the proposed labeling that “Administer ZILBRYSQ subcutaneously into areas of the abdomen, thighs, or back of the upper arms.” For further details, refer to Section 14.2.5.
L553: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L554: ## 8.2.1. Drug Interactions
L555: Zilucoplan is a 15-amino acid macrocyclic peptide; hence in vivo degradation into smaller peptide fragments and amino acids by nonspecific proteases is likely. Hydrolysis and terminal \omega-oxidation were identified as the two major metabolic pathways of zilucoplan resulting in the formation of RA102758 and RA103488, respectively. RA102758 is formed by protease mediated degradation of the c terminal lysine residue attached to the ethylene glycol-palmitoyl tail. The metabolite RA103488 is formed by \omega-hydroxylation on the palmitoyl tail of intact zilucoplan majorly by cytochrome P450 (CYP) 4F2 (minor contribution by CYP4A11,
L556: CYP4F3A, and CYP4F3B). The exposure of the aforementioned two metabolites is low compared to zilucoplan (i.e., ~10% of the zilucoplan exposure at steady state).
L557: In vitro studies have shown that zilucoplan is not a substrate of major cytochrome P450 (CYP450) enzymes (1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6 and 3A) and transporters (P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3). In vitro studies also suggested lack of potential for zilucoplan to inhibit or induce CYP450 or inhibit uridine diphosphoglucuronosyl transferases or inhibit major transporters. From the results of in vitro drug interaction testing, the Applicant concluded that clinically relevant interactions between zilucoplan and substrates of major CYP450 enzymes (1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, 3A, and 4F), uridine diphosphoglucuronosyl transferases (1A1, 1A3, 1A4, 1A6, 1A9, 2B7, and 2B15), and transporters (P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, OATP1B3, OAT1, OAT3, MATE1, MATE2-K, OCT1, and OCT2) is unlikely (Section 14.1). Clinical drug-drug interaction (DDI) studies were therefore not conducted.
L558: For gMG patients, concomitant medications that inhibit/inducer CYP4F2 are unlikely. Further, CYP4F2 is majorly involved in disposition of endogenous long-chain fatty acids and is not known as a major CYP enzyme contributing to disposition of many prescription drugs. Therefore, for zilucoplan, clinical DDI victim study with CYP4F2 perpetrator is not necessary. Additionally, there is no established CYP4F2 perpetrator/inhibitor that can be used for victim study for CYP4F2 substrate. Based on the above, a clinical DDI study for zilucoplan is not warranted.
L559: Overall, due to its lack of interactions with transporters and major CYP enzymes in vitro, the potential of zilucoplan for drug interactions is low; therefore, no dose adjustments are proposed for use of zilucoplan with concomitant medications. The review team agrees with the Applicant's conclusions.
L560: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L561: Zilucoplan was granted orphan drug designation; therefore, pediatric assessments under the Pediatric Research Equity Act are not required. However, the Applicant has submitted protocols for pediatric studies.
L562: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L563: There are limited data for the use of zilucoplan in pregnant and lactating women, as subjects of childbearing potential were required to use a highly effective method of birth control, and the study drug was discontinued if subjects were pregnant per the protocol.
L564: There was one subject (b) (6) who became pregnant as of the clinical cutoff date. The pregnancy occurred in a subject who was on placebo in the double-blind phase of Study MG0009, and zilucoplan 0.1 mg/kg in the MG0009 extension. Reportedly, zilucoplan was discontinued one day after the last menstrual period. The subject was exposed to zilucoplan for "more than a year." The pregnancy was complicated by gestational diabetes, and the infant was delivered by elective C-section at full term. By the time of follow-up of the infant at about 16
L565: months of age, there were no reported congenital malformations, failure to thrive, or developmental delay.
L566: In an enhanced pre- and postnatal development study with an embryofetal component in cynomolgus monkeys, SC administration of zilucoplan (0, 1, 2, or 4 mg/kg/day) to pregnant dams from GD 20 through 100 produced no adverse effects on pregnancy outcome or embryofetal development in groups evaluated on PND 100, but in the groups dosed from GD 20 through birth, there was an increase in prenatal loss (spontaneous abortion and unscheduled C-section due to intrauterine death) in all treatment groups compared to C (6.25, 31.25, 25, and 31.25% pregnancy loss in C, LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively). Complement inhibition was similar at all doses throughout the dosing period, which could account for the lack of a dose-response. At the low-effect dose for increased embryofetal mortality, the maternal plasma exposure (AUC) was 230 ug.h/mL.
L567: In a male fertility study in the cynomolgus monkey, SC administration of zilucoplan (0, 1, 2, or 4 mg/kg/day) for 13 weeks did not produce mortality, clinical signs, or effects on BW, testicular size, clinical pathology, immunophenotyping, or organ weights. Testicular germ cell depletion/degeneration was observed in treated animal from all dose groups at the terminal and recovery sacrifices, although not with a dose-related incidence or severity. The finding was said to be consistent with a spontaneous background lesion, but since it only occurred in drug-treated animals, relationship to drug cannot be dismissed. Inhibition of complement-dependent hemolysis was maintained throughout the dosing period at similar levels in all treatment groups. At the LOAEL for germ cell degeneration, exposures (AUC) were 227 and 488 ug.hr/mL on days 1 and 91, respectively.

As such OPQ recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective.
L570: For additional details, refer to the OPQ Integrated Quality Review dated June 6, 2023, and review addendum dated August 23, 2023.

L577: ## III. Additional Analyses and Information
L578: ## 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L579: Zilucoplan (also referred to as RA101495), is a macrocyclic peptide that is intended to inhibit complement activation at the level of complement component 5 (C5) by binding to C5 and preventing cleavage into C5a and C5b. Ra Pharmaceuticals submitted the initial IND 134340 on September 1, 2017, and the IND became active on September 29, 2017, for the treatment of generalized myasthenia gravis (gMG).
L580: In the end-of-phase 2 (EOP2) meeting preliminary comments, dated March 29, 2019, the Agency provided advice on dosing regimen, endpoint selection, immunogenicity assessment, drug-drug interaction assessments, metabolites, and recommendations on the human factors validation and use-related risk analysis.
L581: Orphan drug designation was granted on August 26, 2019, for the treatment of myasthenia gravis. With the orphan drug designation, the Applicant was exempt from Pediatric Research Equity Act requirements.
L582: The Applicant submitted a human factors validation protocol for review on December 20, 2019. An advice letter detailing issues and recommendations on the human factors validation study protocol was provided on February 14, 2020.
L583: A proprietary name review request for “Zilbrysq” was submitted to the IND on July 14, 2020, and the name was determined to be conditionally acceptable on December 22, 2020. The same proposed proprietary name was submitted to the initial NDA.
L584: Type C written responses were provided on September 13, 2021, providing guidance and advice on the statistical analysis plan for the Phase 3 MG0010 study and the integrated statistical analysis plan.
L585: The Agency notified the Applicant in an email on August 16, 2021, that the Applicant will need to conduct an in vivo micronucleus assay and provide a weight-of-evidence assessment of the carcinogenic potential of zilucoplan at the time of NDA submission. The micronucleus assay and carcinogenicity assessment were submitted in the initial NDA.
L586: A Type B, pre-NDA meeting was scheduled for May 5, 2022, and subsequently cancelled by the Applicant since the Applicant had no additional questions or clarifications of the May 3, 2022, preliminary comments. In the preliminary comments, the Division requested that the Applicant clarify how they intended to meet the substantial evidence of effectiveness standard with a single pivotal study.
L587: In the pre-NDA meeting package, the Applicant proposed a safety database of 84 patients exposed for at least 12 months of the proposed dose (0.3 mg/kg/d); however, the Division typically requires a minimum of 100 patients for a rare disease such as gMG. In an email correspondence on May 4, 2022, the Applicant proposed safety data for 90 gMG patients with at
L588: least 1 year of exposure at the time of initial NDA submission and an estimated 38 gMG patients at the 120-day safety update.
L589: Although the proposed Risk Evaluation and Mitigation Strategy (REMS) to mitigate the potential risk of meningococcal infection was not submitted with the pre-NDA document, the Agency provided recommendations what to include in the proposed REMS in the NDA. The proposed REMS was submitted with the initial NDA.
L590: The original NDA was submitted on August 31, 2022, and filed on October 30, 2022, with no filing issues identified.
L591: ## 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L592: ## 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L593: The in vitro metabolic profiling of zilucoplan indicated that in addition to parent, 6 metabolites were produced in monkey and human hepatocytes. No human unique metabolite was observed. In vivo, two major metabolic pathways, hydrolysis, and terminal \omega-oxidation, resulted in the formation of RA102758 and RA103488, respectively, in similar proportions in monkey and human plasma. These metabolites are covered in the monkey toxicity studies; however, according to the FDA clinical pharmacology reviewer, there are no major human metabolites.
L594: Based on the pharmacologic and metabolic similarity to humans, the cynomolgus macaque was considered the most relevant species for toxicity testing; therefore, nonclinical safety assessment in two species was not required by the Division. Repeat-dose toxicity studies consisted of 4-week SC studies in the rat and cynomolgus monkey and 13- and 39-week SC studies in the monkey. In the 4-week rat study, adverse effects were limited to injection site reactions at the HD (40 mg/kg/day). In the 4-week monkey study (0, 1, 2, or 4 mg/kg/day), elevated bile acids and lipase and peribiliary mixed cell inflammation in the liver were observed at the MD and HD. Widespread inflammatory effects and liver and pancreas histopathology were highlighted in the reviews of the 13-week monkey study (0, 0.25, 1, 2, or 10 mg/kg/day). In the pharmacology/toxicology review under [REDACTED](b) (4) (Rama Guidi, Ph.D., December 29, 2016), the histopathological changes observed in the 13-week study included:
L595: ...marked inflammation (vasculitis), mild pancreatic acinar degeneration, mild ductular hyperplasia, and degeneration of the pancreas in one male monkey administered 10 mg/kg/day, and minimal pancreatic acinar degeneration at \ge 1 mg/kg/day in females. Moderate mucosal vesicular degeneration of the tongue was present in two 10 mg/kg/day males, one of which had minimal neutrophilic inflammation of the tongue mucosa in an area of vesiculation. Minimal vascular/perivascular mononuclear cell infiltration of the tongue and urinary bladder was observed at 0.25 mg/kg/day. Vascular/perivascular mononuclear cell infiltration showed partial recovery, however microscopic changes in the pancreas and liver at 10 mg/kg/day did not fully resolve after 4 weeks of observation.
L596: According to the pharmacology/toxicology review under [REDACTED](b) (4) (Wei Sun, Ph.D., April 22, 2019):
L597: There were RA101495-related clinical signs for males in the high dose [10 mg/kg/day] group that included reddened and dry face, bilateral ocular discharge with periorbital redness and dry face, slight facial jaundice (icterus), and abdominal pain. Increased ALT, ASP, ALP, GGT, creatine kinase, total bilirubin, lipase, and bile acids were observed in males from the 10 mg/kg/day group. These findings, with particular reference to bilirubin, appeared to correlate with findings of slight facial jaundice (icterus). Potential target organs of toxicity included the tongue (moderate mucosal vesicular degeneration of the tongue for males at 10 mg/kg/day), pancreas (marked vascular/perivascular mononuclear cell inflammation (vasculitis), mild pancreatic acinar degeneration, and mild ductular hyperplasia for males at 10 mg/kg/day), and multi-organ mononuclear infiltrates observed in the pancreas, kidney, tongue, esophagus, salivary gland, thyroid, rectum, stomach, and urinary bladder for all drug treated groups. ...The marked vascular/perivascular mononuclear inflammation (vasculitis) in the pancreas at the high dose of 10 mg/kg/day was considered adverse and dose-limiting.
L598: These observations are consistent with the findings in the 39-week monkey study (0, 0.25, 1, 2, 4, or 6 mg/kg/day) in which all doses were associated with swelling, ocular discharge, reddened skin, dose site reactions, and inflammatory cell infiltrates of most organs. Mononuclear and/or mixed cell interface infiltrates and epithelial vesicular degeneration/erosions/necrosis were prominent in the tongue, cervix, vagina, esophagus, stomach, and skin. Other microscopic findings included salivary gland duct degeneration/regeneration, uterine endometrial degeneration, increased cellularity of the thymus, and epithelial vesicular degeneration in the cervix. In 3 animals that were sacrificed early due to moribund condition (LD animal 2501 on Day 245 with colitis, MHD (4 mg/kg) and HD animals 5003 and 6503 on Days 52 and 203 with skin ulcerations and erosions) histopathology findings included liver fibrosis and lung edema (5003) and moderate pancreatic acinar degeneration (all 3 animals) or pancreatic duct degeneration/regeneration with acinar atrophy (2501), with associated increases in amylase and lipase at the 2 highest doses. Pancreatic acinar cell degeneration with fibrosis/fibroplasia was also found in 1 MD (2 mg/kg) animal (4003) at the scheduled terminal sacrifice. After the recovery period, inflammatory cell infiltration had regressed in most organs, but mucosal infiltrates of the esophagus, squamous metaplasia of the cervical mucosa, increased medullary cellularity in the thymus, and lymphoid follicles or aggregates in the bone marrow remained, although decreased in incidence and/or severity. A NOAEL was not established, but the highest tolerated dose was considered to be 2 mg/kg/day, which was associated with an exposure (AUC0-24) of 541 µg.h/mL for combined sexes on Day 273, approximately 2X the maximal anticipated clinical exposure (approximately 275 ug*hr/mL at 32.4 mg).
L599: The pancreatic infiltrates and degeneration, lymphoid hypercellularity, and sporadic bridging bile duct hyperplasia and hepatic fibrosis observed the monkey studies were thought to be secondary to pathogen proliferation, colonization, and infection resulting from immunosuppression caused by pharmacologic inhibition of C5. Opportunistic pathogens were isolated in cultures collected from 2 animals sacrificed prematurely in the 39-week monkey study. Since blocking C5 cleavage prevents the formation of the terminal pathway products C5a and C5b-9, this would be expected to increase the risk of infections.
L600: Zilucoplan was negative for genotoxicity in vitro (Ames and human lymphocyte) and in vivo (rat micronucleus) assays. No carcinogenicity studies were conducted, as agreed to by the Division, because of the lack of pharmacological activity in rodent species.
L601: In a male fertility study in the cynomolgus monkey, SC administration of zilucoplan (0, 1, 2, or 4 mg/kg/day) for 13 weeks did not produce mortality, clinical signs, or effects on BW, testicular size, clinical pathology, immunophenotyping, or organ weights. Testicular germ cell depletion/degeneration was observed in treated animal from all dose groups at the terminal and recovery sacrifices, although not with a dose-related incidence or severity. The finding was said to be consistent with a spontaneous background lesion, but since it only occurred in drug-treated animals, relationship to drug cannot be dismissed. Inhibition of complement-dependent hemolysis was maintained throughout the dosing period at similar levels in all treatment groups. At the LOAEL for germ cell degeneration, exposures (AUC) were 227 and 488 ug.hr/mL on Days 1 and 91, respectively.
L602: In an enhanced pre- and postnatal development study with an embryofetal component in cynomolgus monkeys, SC administration of zilucoplan (0, 1, 2, or 4 mg/kg/day) to pregnant dams from GD 20 through 100 produced no adverse effects on pregnancy outcome or embryofetal development in groups evaluated on PND 100, but in the groups dosed from GD 20 through birth, there was an increase in prenatal loss in all treatment groups compared to C (6.25, 31.25, 25, and 31.25% pregnancy loss in C, LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively). Complement inhibition was similar at all doses throughout the dosing period, which could account for the lack of a dose-response. At the low-effect dose for increased embryofetal mortality, the maternal plasma exposure (AUC) was 230 ug.h/mL.
L603: The nonclinical studies completed for the NDA are considered adequate to support approval. The reproductive (testicular germ cell depletion/degeneration) and developmental (increased prenatal loss) effects observed in monkeys should be described in labeling.

L617: The following endpoints were evaluated: clinical signs, BW, food consumption, veterinary physical examinations, cardiac (ECG, BP, and HR), ophthalmology, clinical pathology (hematology, coagulation, clinical chemistry, and urinalysis), ADA (direct enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay), TK, organ weights, and gross and microscopic findings.
L618: ## Observations and Results
L619: ## **Mortality**
L620: Single animals from the 2 highest dose groups were sacrificed early (Days 52 and 203) due to multifocal skin erosions and/or ulcerations. A single LD animal was sacrificed on Day 245 due to weight loss, liquid feces, abdominal pain, and anorexia that correlated histologically with chronic ulcerative colitis and proctitis.
L621: ## **Clinical Signs**
L622: Clinical signs included swelling, ocular discharge, reddened skin, injection site reactions, and various lesions or abrasions, which were observed in all treatment groups. Dose holidays were given for 1 MHD animal and 1 HD animal from Days 148 to 152 for buccal mucosa erythema and oral ulceration which resolved by Day 152.
L623: NDA 216834
L624: Zilucoplan
L625: ## **Body Weights**
L626: There were no drug-related changes in BW, except in the LD animal (2501) that was sacrificed early.
L627: ## **Cardiovascular**
L628: There were no effects on cardiovascular endpoints.
L629: ## **Ophthalmology**
L630: One HD male (6504) had diffuse conjunctival swelling and conjunctival hyperemia on Days 69 and 268 that resolved after the 8-week recovery period. Gingivitis was also noted concurrently in this animal. Dose-dependent drug concentrations were measured in the tear and salivary fluid, and HD animals had concentrations high enough to inhibit local C5. Therefore, the immune response in the mucous membranes of the eye and oral cavity may have been compromised.
L631: ## **Clinical Pathology**
L632: ## Hematology
L633: There were no drug-related changes in hematology or coagulation parameters.
L634: ## Clinical chemistry
L635: Drug-related findings included increased amylase and lipase in individual animals (5002, 5003, 6503) at the 2 highest doses. In the 1 MHD male (5003) that was sacrificed prematurely, clinical chemistry changes (increased liver enzymes, bilirubin, and bile acids) correlated with histopathology findings in the bile duct and liver (bridging portal fibrosis with biliary hyperplasia, portal mononuclear cell infiltrates, and edema of the liver).
L636: ## Urinalysis
L637: There were no changes in urinalysis parameters.
L638: ## **Complement Activity**
L639: Suppression of hemolysis was observed throughout the dosing period at all doses, returning to normal during the recovery period.
L640: ## **ADA**
L641: Serum collected during the dosing and recovery periods was negative for anti-drug immunoreactivity.
L642: ## **Toxicokinetics**
L643: TK data are shown in Table 38.

L648: ## Organ weights/Gross pathology
L649: There were no drug-related gross observations or organ weight differences on Days 274 (termination) or 330 (recovery).
L650: ## Histopathology
L651: Treatment-related histopathology findings at terminal necropsy consisted predominantly of inflammatory cell infiltrates of various organs at all doses, with generally a dose-related incidence and severity. Mononuclear and/or mixed cell interface infiltrates and epithelial vesicular degeneration/erosions/necrosis were prominent in the tongue, cervix, vagina, esophagus, stomach, and skin. Other findings included salivary gland duct degeneration/regeneration, uterine endometrial degeneration, increased cellularity of the thymus, and epithelial vesicular degeneration in the cervix accompanied by increased incidence and severity of squamous metaplasia and epithelial erosions (Table 39).
L652: In the 3 animals sacrificed prematurely (LD animal 2501 with colitis, MHD and HD animals 5003 and 6503 with skin ulcerations and erosions), histopathology findings included liver fibrosis with biliary duct degeneration and lung edema (5003) and moderate pancreatic acinar degeneration (all 3 animals) or pancreatic duct degeneration/regeneration with acinar atrophy (2501). One MD animal (4003) had pancreatic acinar cell degeneration with fibrosis/fibroplasia, similar to that noted in animals sacrificed prematurely.
L653: After the recovery period, inflammatory cell infiltrates of most organs and the administration sites were considered completely recovered; however, mucosal infiltrates of the esophagus, squamous metaplasia of the cervical mucosa, increased medullary cellularity in the thymus, and
L654: lymphoid follicles or aggregates in the bone marrow remained present although decreased in incidence and/or severity (Table 40).

L677: Endpoints consisted of mortality, clinical observations, BW, complement activity (ex vivo pharmacodynamic assay at 1, 2, 4, 6, and 24 hours on day 1, week 13, and weeks 4 and 8 of recovery), testicular evaluation (ultrasound during weeks 1, 7, and 13 of the dosing period and the last week of the recovery period), clinical pathology (hematology and coagulation, clinical chemistry, and urinalysis), immunophenotyping, macroscopic and microscopic findings, organ weights, analysis of spermatogenic stages, and immunohistochemistry were evaluated.
L678: The HD was based on previous studies in cynomolgus monkeys in which hepatobiliary toxicity (cholestasis) was observed. The LD was anticipated to result in approximately 1.5X clinical therapeutic levels. All doses were expected to result in complete inhibition of complement activity at the C5 level.

L683: ## Observations and Results
L684: ## **Mortality**
L685: There were no unscheduled deaths.
L686: ## **Clinical Signs**
L687: There were no drug-related clinical observations.
L688: ## **Body Weights**
L689: There were no drug-related differences in BW or BW change.
L690: ## **Food Consumption**
L691: There was no effect on food consumption.
L692: ## **Complement Activity**
L693: Complement-dependent hemolysis was maintained below approximately 5% for 5 of the 6 LD animals and for all MD and HD animals throughout the 13-week dosing period. Little to no inhibition of hemolysis remained at the end of the recovery period.
L694: ## **Testicular size**
L695: There were no clearly drug-related differences in testicular parameters. Over the course of the treatment period, testicular volume increased in C and MD males and decreased in LD and HD males (Table 44). One HD male (P0303) showed a permanent decrease in testicular size during the dosing period and reduced testicular weight at necropsy.

L712: ## Macroscopic
L713: There were no notable macroscopic findings.
L714: ## Microscopic
L715: Testicular germ cell depletion/degeneration (minimal to slight, unilateral or bilateral) was observed in 2 LD (P0104, P0106), 3 MD (P0203, P0204, and P0206), and 2 HD animals (P0303, P0304). Testicular fibrosis was also observed in animal P0304 (Table 46). According to the pathology report: “Degeneration/depletion of germ cells in the testicular tubules were characterized by various combinations of tubular dilatation, tubular vacuolation, reduced height of germinal epithelium, reduced spermatogenesis stages, and tubuli with sertoli cells only.” It was reported that “spermatogenesis was complete, and all spermatogenic stages were present in the testes of all animals.”

L733: ## Immunohistochemistry
L734: When thymus tissue was examined for immune composition by immunohistochemistry [mC]) using mC-specific for CD3, CD4, CD8, CD20, and CD45, there was a minimal overall decrease in the number/distribution of CD3, CD4, and CD8 positive cells in HD animals, at both the terminal and recovery sacrifices. There were no clear effects on thymic CD20+ve, or CD45+ve cells at any dose. An increase in the incidence and severity of thymic involution/atrophy/lymphoid depletion was noted in HD animals.
L735: ## Toxicokinetics
L736: Exposure increased with increasing dose in an approximately dose-proportional manner (Table 49). Absorption of zilucoplan was prolonged, with Tmax values observed between 1 and 6 hours postdose. Approximately 2-fold accumulation was observed over the 13-week dosing period. T1/2 could only be calculated in recovery animals and ranged from approximately 79 hours to 108 hours and decreased with increasing dose.

L822: ## **Pregnancy Outcome**
L823: Prenatal loss, detected by ultrasound, was increased compared to C in all treatment groups: 1/16 (6.25%) C, 5/16 LD (31.25%), 4/16 (25.0%), and 5/16 (31.25%) HD dams experienced pregnancy loss prior to parturition, not counting stillbirth (Table 57 and Table 58). (Fetal loss prior to GD 140 in cynomolgus monkeys is typically defined as abortion, that after GD 140 as stillbirth.) Embryofetal deaths occurred throughout the dosing period in drug-treated groups.
L824: Stillbirths (infants died prior or during birth and their lungs were not inflated; stillbirth and infant death considered postnatal losses in study report) were observed in 3/15 (20.0%) C, 1/11 (9.1%) LD, 2/12 (16.7%) MD, and 1/11 (9.1%) HD pregnancies (while all C stillbirths occurred after GD 140, 1 MD and 1 HD stillbirth occurred earlier). The number of live born infants was 12, 10, 10, and 10 in C, LD, MD, and HD groups, respectively.

L863: ## Macroscopic Observations
L864: There were no unusual findings at the terminal necropsy. Among the 7 offspring that died during the postnatal observation period, a heart malformation was found in 1 HD female that was sacrificed moribund on PND 16 (71254F-1). Although heart malformations are rare (12 cases in 947 control fetuses/infants in lab historical data), this was not considered drug-related in the study report due to the isolated nature of the finding. However, because it occurred at the HD and not all offspring that died early were examined, it should be considered of uncertain relation to treatment.

L876: ## **Methods and Results**
L877: In the dose range-finding study (250, 500, or 2000 mg/kg), the MTD was estimated to be 500 mg/kg based on clinical signs at the HD (male: rough coat and hunched posture, male and female: large lesion in the belly which was dark stained and hairless). In the micronucleus assay, SD rats (5 males/dose) were administered zilucoplan twice (at a 24 h interval) by SC injection and euthanized 48 hours after the first dosing for extraction of the bone marrow. RA101495 did not induce a significant increase in micronuclei in bone (Table 63).

L888: ## 14. Clinical Pharmacology

The plasma protein binding for zilucoplan, RA102758, and RA103488 was &gt;99%.

L903: ## 14.1.3. Metabolism of Zilucoplan (Study # 16900, # TR-0160, # TR-0128)
L904: Metabolite radioprofiling and identification and cytochrome P450 (CYP) phenotyping assay indicated two major metabolites, RA102758 (formed by protease mediated degradation), and RA103488 (formed by \omega-oxidation by CYP4F2)
L905: Based on metabolite radioprofiling and identification, zilucoplan was stable in human plasma with no metabolite detection up to 8-hour incubation (Study # 16900). The in vitro metabolic profile of zilucoplan evaluated in human hepatocytes indicated peptide hydrolysis as the predominant metabolic pathway. The CYP phenotyping assay in human liver microsomes and recombinant human CYP enzymes indicated that RA103488, the \omega-oxidation of zilucoplan, is mainly due to CYP4F2, with minor contributions from CYP4A11, CYP4F3A, and CYP4F3B (Study # TR-0160). Quantitation of metabolites in human plasma (# TR-0128) from First-In-Human (FIH) study (# UP0112) indicated two major metabolites, RA102758 and RA103488. RA102758 is formed by protease mediated degradation of the peptide chain at the terminal amino acid lysine attached to ethylene glycol24-palmitic acid tail of zilucoplan. Whereas RA103488 is formed by \omega-hydroxylation of the C16 lipid tail of the parent drug by CYP4F2. It should be noted that the systemic exposure of RA102758 and RA103488 was 10.6% and 9.4% of the systemic exposure of zilucoplan at steady state (0.3 mg/kg/day for 14 days, study # UP0113) indicating overall low exposure of these two metabolites in humans.
L906: ## 14.1.4. Inhibition and Induction of CYP Enzymes by Zilucoplan and Metabolite RA102758 (Study # TR-0102, # 18RAPH3, #20RAPH3, # 20RAPH5R3)
L907: The in vitro studies as discussed below suggested that zilucoplan and its metabolite RA102758 is not an inhibitor or inducer of major CYP enzymes.
L908: The CYP inhibition potential of zilucoplan and RA102758 (up to a concentration of 100\muM) was evaluated for major CYP enzymes and CYP4F was evaluated in vitro in human liver microsomes (Study # TR-0102, # 18RAPH3, #20RAPH3). Per the results presented in Table 66 (I/Ki for all CYPs less than &lt;0.02), drug-drug interaction due to CYP inhibition by zilucoplan or RA102758 is unlikely. The half maximal inhibitory concentration (IC_{50}) shift assay (#20RAPH3) results also indicated that there was no time-dependent inhibition of major CYP enzymes (1A2, 2B6, 2C8, 2C9, 2C19, 2D6, and 3A) by zilucoplan or RA102758. The potential of zilucoplan to induce CYP enzymes (CYP 1A2, 2B6, and 3A4) was evaluated using human hepatocytes (Table 67). Since there was no increase in mRNA level for the tested CYPs and the fold-change was below twofold, it was concluded that zilucoplan is unlikely an inducer of CYP enzymes tested. The review team agrees with the Applicant's conclusions.
L909: The CYP inhibition and induction potential of metabolite RA103488 was not evaluated. However, it is noted that RA103488 is formed by hydroxylation of the C16 lipid tail of zilucoplan and is therefore structurally similar and is not expected to have a different CYP inhibition or induction profile compared to zilucoplan. Further, the systemic exposure of the metabolite RA103488 is ~10% to that of zilucoplan. The induction potential of metabolite
L910: RA102758 was also not evaluated; however, it is not concerning since the systemic exposure of the RA102758 is also low ~10% to that of zilucoplan.

L923: ## 14.1.5. Inhibition of Uridine 5'-Diphospho-Glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) Enzymes (Study # TR-0102)
L924: Based on in vitro studies, any clinically relevant interaction between zilucoplan and uridine diphosphoglucuronosyl transferases (UGT) substrate is not expected.
L925: UGT inhibition by zilucoplan (up to 100\muM) was evaluated on UGT 1A1, 1A3, 1A4, 1A6, 1A9, 2B7, and 2B15 human recombinant UGT enzymes (Table 68). A weak inhibition was observed for UGT1A1 (IC50 12\muM) and 1A3 (IC50 48\muM), while no inhibition greater than 50% was detected for other isoforms at 100\muM. These results indicated that a clinically relevant interaction between zilucoplan and substrates of these UGT isoforms is unlikely. The review team agrees with the Applicant's conclusions.

L930: ## 14.1.6. Interaction Potential of Zilucoplan and RA102758 for Drug Transporters (Study # TR-0100, #TR-0101, # TR-0145, Rapharma-12-23Jan2018, and Rapharma-16-22Aug2019)
L931: In vitro studies suggested that zilucoplan is not a substrate of P-gp, BCRP, OATP1B1, or OATP1B3 (Table 69). The substrate potential of zilucoplan for renal transporters (OAT1, OAT3, MATE1/MATE2-K, OCT2) is not evaluated. It is noted that excretion of zilucoplan and its metabolites is negligible (&lt;1% of the dose) and hence lack of these data are not concerning. Based on the above, it is unlikely that zilucoplan will be a victim of inhibitor or inducer of the above transporters. The potential of zilucoplan to inhibit major transporters is low since unbound maximum plasma concentration (Cmax,unbound)/IC50 ratio was lower than 0.02 (Table 69). The review team agrees with the Applicant's conclusion that a clinically relevant drug-drug interaction due to transporter inhibition by zilucoplan or RA102758 is not expected.

L938: ## 14.2. In Vivo Studies
L939: ## 14.2.1. Study # UP0112 – Single Ascending Dose and Multiple Dose Study
L940: ## Study Design
L941: The study UP0112 was a Phase 1, randomized, placebo-controlled, double-blind, single ascending dose and multiple dose study for the evaluation of safety, tolerability pharmacokinetics (PK), and pharmacodynamics (PD) of zilucoplan in healthy subjects. The single dose cohorts evaluated zilucoplan (n=10 subjects) versus placebo (n=8 subjects) at 4 dose levels (0.05 mg/kg (n=2 subjects), 0.1 mg/kg (n=4 subjects), 0.2 mg/kg (n=4 subjects), and
L942: 0.4 mg/kg (n=4 subjects)) administered subcutaneously (SC). The multiple dose cohort evaluated zilucoplan (n=4 subjects) at 0.2 mg/kg administered subcutaneously once daily over 7 days versus placebo (n=2 subjects). Blood samples were collected predose and postdose for the determination of plasma concentrations of zilucoplan and its metabolites (RA102758 and RA103488) and for the measurement of complement activity (sheep red blood cell [sRBC] lysis) and total C5 levels. For single dose cohorts, blood samples for PK were obtained on days 1, 2, 5, 8, 15, 29. Day 1 included collection of samples predose and at 15 minutes, 1-, 3-, 6- and 12-hours postdose. For the multiple dose cohort, blood samples for PK were obtained on day 1 through 7 and at days 8, 15, 21 and 35. days 1 to 7 included a predose collection and at 3 and 6 hours postdose. PD sample collection occurred concurrently with collection of PK samples. All subjects completed the study per protocol.
L943: ## Results
L944: Table 70 and Table 71 presents zilucoplan PK parameters following single and multiple doses respectively. The exposure of metabolites (RA102758 and RA103488) relative to zilucoplan is presented in Table 72.

L959: ## **Key Conclusions**
L960: From single ascending dose cohorts, the peak exposure maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) and systemic exposure (area under the concentration-time curve, AUC_{0-24}) increased with the increase in dose. The increase in C_{max} was approximately dose proportional while the increase in AUC was less than dose proportional between 0.05 mg/kg and 0.40 mg/kg. The peak exposure occurred at time ranging from 3 hours to 4.6 hours across all doses. The mean terminal half-life (t_{1/2}) ranged from 155.6 hours to 185.4 hours (i.e., approximately 7 days) across all doses.
L961: From multiple dose cohort, following daily dosing of zilucoplan (0.2 mg/kg) for 7 days, C_{max} and AUC_{tau} (area under the concentration-time curve over the dosing interval of 24 hours) increased by approximately threefold. The terminal half-life (t_{1/2}) of zilucoplan at 0.2 mg/kg dose was consistent following single (t_{1/2}=172 hours) and repeat dosing (t_{1/2}=161.9 hours). Steady state was not achieved with 7 days of daily dosing at 0.2 mg/kg; however, based on multiple once daily doses at 0.3 mg/kg in Phase 3 study MG0010, steady state trough concentration of zilucoplan reached by 4 weeks. The systemic exposure (AUC_{0-24}) of metabolites, RA102758 and RA103488, was 12% and 16% respectively when compared to the systemic exposure of zilucoplan.
L962: The Applicant noted that across all dose groups, plasma levels of total C5 increased following a single dose and multiple doses. The mean maximum total C5 levels reached 81\mug/mL, 99\mug/mL, 99\mug/mL, 116 \mug/mL postdose following single doses of 0.05, 0.1, 0.20, and 0.40 mg/kg respectively. There were no notable changes observed in C5 concentration across the time points for the placebo groups. For the multiple dose (MD) cohort, 0.2 mg/kg for 7 days, the mean maximum total C5 levels reached 151\mug/mL. The observed increase in C5 is due to the prolongation of the half-life of C5 bound to zilucoplan and not due to increase in available free C5. The Applicant noted that the increases in C5 level coincided with increased plasma level of zilucoplan.
L963: For complement inhibition, the Applicant found that the mean maximum percent inhibition of sRBC lysis compared to baseline reached 52%, 86%, 87%, and 95% postdose following single doses of 0.05, 0.1, 0.2, and 0.4 mg/kg respectively suggesting dose-dependent complement inhibition. Mean sRBC lysis levels remained below baseline for up to 2 days in the 0.05 mg/kg cohort, up to 4 days in the 0.1 mg/kg cohort, and for up to 7 days in the 0.2 and 0.4 mg/kg cohorts. There was no inhibition of sRBC lysis for the pooled placebo group across all time points. For MD cohort, 0.2 mg/kg for 7 days, the mean percent inhibition of sRBC lysis reached &gt;95% after first dosing and continued through the 7 days of dosing. Mean sRBC lysis levels remained below baseline for up to 15 days in the 0.2 mg/kg for MD cohort. It was also noted that the maximum %sRBC lysis inhibition i.e., peak PD effect coincided with the time for peak concentration (t_{max}) of zilucoplan for both single doses and multiple dose cohorts. The overall PK and PD data from this Phase 1 study suggested correlation between PK and complement inhibition.
L964: ## 14.2.2. Study # UP0113 – Ethno-Bridging Study in Japanese and Caucasian Subjects
L965: ## Study Design
L966: Study UP0113 was a Phase 1, randomized, double-blind study to investigate the safety, tolerability, PK, and PD of single and multiple doses of zilucoplan following SC administration in healthy Japanese and Caucasian subjects. The study consisted of 2 single-dose cohorts (0.1 mg/kg and 0.3 mg/kg; zilucoplan n=4 each, Japanese and white in each cohort; matching placebo n=1 each, Japanese and white in each cohort) and a multiple-dose cohort (0.3 mg/kg; once daily (QD) for 14 days; zilucoplan n=6 each, Japanese and white; matching placebo n=2 each Japanese and white). Blood samples for the determination of plasma concentration of zilucoplan and its metabolites (RA102758 and RA103488) and for the measurement of complement activity (sRBC lysis) and total C5 levels were collected predose, postdose (1, 3, 6, 12, and 24 hours), at study visits (days 7 and 14) post-treatment, and end-of-study (EOS) visit. All subjects completed the study. The Applicant submitted updated PK analysis for zilucoplan, RA1027528, and RA103488 in sequence number (SN)# 0037 upon correction related to the unit of time and PK samples recorded as BLQ.
L967: ## Results
L968: The PK comparison of zilucoplan, RA102758, and RA103488 between white and Japanese subjects following single and multiple doses are presented in Table 73, Table 74, Table 75, and Table 76.

L1003: ## **Key Conclusions**
L1004: Geometric mean concentrations over time of zilucoplan, RA103488, and RA102758 and the statistical analyses to assess the effect of ethnicity on exposure parameters (Cmax and AUC) indicated that PK was generally similar for Japanese and white subjects following single (0.1 mg/kg and 0.3 mg/kg) and multiple (0.3 mg/kg, QD for 14 days) doses of zilucoplan. The updated PK analysis provided by the Applicant (SN # 0037) for zilucoplan (single dose and multiple dose), RA102758 (multiple dose), and RA103488 (multiple dose) did not impact the conclusion for the ethnicity assessment. The exposure of the 2 metabolites is low compared to zilucoplan (White ----- RA102758 – 10.6% of zilucoplan exposure; RA103488 – 9.4% of zilucoplan exposure; Japanese ----- RA102758 – 12.6% of zilucoplan exposure; RA103488 – 6.9% of zilucoplan exposure). The PD effects (total C5 levels, and %RBC lysis) of zilucoplan was also similar between Japanese and white subjects following single (0.1 mg/kg and 0.3 mg/kg) and multiple (0.3 mg/kg, QD for 14 days.) doses of zilucoplan. It is noted that any labeling claim based on the above ethnicity assessment is not made by the Applicant.
L1005: ## **14.2.3. Study # UP0114 – Renal Impairment Study**
L1006: ## **Study Design**
L1007: Study UP0114 was a Phase 1, open-label study to assess the PK of zilucoplan and its metabolites (RA102758 and RA103488) in subjects with severe renal impairment (estimated creatinine clearance &lt;30 mL/min based on Cockcroft-Gault formula; n=8) and matched (with respect to age, sex, body mass index) healthy subjects with normal renal function (n=8) after administration of a single subcutaneous (SC) dose of 0.3 mg/kg. Blood samples for PK analysis were collected predose and at 1, 2, 4, 8, 12, 48, 72, 96, and 120 hours after the SC injection, as well as at day 15, 22, 29, and 36 visits. Pooled urine collections were tested for concentrations of zilucoplan and its metabolites at predose, 0 to 14 hours, 24 to 48 hours, and 48 to 120 hours. The PD
L1008: assessment included the measurement of levels of total C5 predose, and at day 3 and day 6 postdose. All subjects completed the study per protocol.
L1009: ## Results
L1010: The PK of zilucoplan, and its metabolites and PD as measured by total C5 levels are summarized in Table 77 and Table 78.

L1020: ## Key Conclusions
L1021: The PK of zilucoplan and metabolite RA102758 is generally similar between subjects with severe renal impairment and healthy subjects. For metabolite RA103488, AUC0-inf and Cmax was 1.4- to 1.5-fold higher in subjects with severe renal impairment respectively as compared to subjects with normal renal function. However, since the exposure of RA103488 is approximately 10% of zilucoplan exposure at steady state (Study # UP0112 and # UP0113), the clinical impact of the observed increase in exposure for RA103488 is expected to be negligible. Further, the total C5 levels were similar across two populations. The urine analysis indicated minimal renal excretion of zilucoplan and metabolites (&lt;1% of dose in total across all analytes). Therefore, the
L1022: review team agrees with the Applicant's labeling proposal that no dose adjustment is required for subjects with renal impairment.
L1023: ## 14.2.4. Study # UP0094 – Hepatic Impairment Study
L1024: ## Study Design
L1025: Study UP0094 was a Phase 1, open-label study to assess the PK of zilucoplan and its metabolites (RA102758 and RA103488) in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment (Child-Pugh category B with a score of 7 to 9; n=8) and matched (with respect to age, sex, body mass index) healthy subjects with normal hepatic function (n=8) after administration of a single subcutaneous (SC) dose of 0.3 mg/kg. Blood samples for PK analysis were collected predose and on days 1, 2 to 6, 15, 22, 29, and 36 after the SC injection. Feces was collected for PK on day 1 and day 2 to day 6. The PD assessment included the measurement of total C5 levels sRBC lysis predose, and 48 hours and 120 hours postdose at day 3, and day 6 postdose and at the EOS. All subjects completed the study per protocol.
L1026: ## Results
L1027: The statistical assessment of the effect of hepatic impairment on PK of zilucoplan and its metabolites are summarized in Table 79, Table 80, and Table 81.

L1052: ## **Key Conclusions**
L1053: The peak exposure Cmax of zilucoplan was similar between moderate hepatic impairment and normal subjects. The systemic exposure (AUC0-inf) of zilucoplan was 24% lower in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment compared with subjects with normal hepatic function and associated increase in clearance is observed. The peak exposure and systemic exposure for both metabolites also increased in moderate hepatic impairment subjects compared to normal subjects ranging from 9% to 22%. The total C5 levels and sRBC lysis was similar across two populations.
L1054: The observed decrease in systemic exposure of zilucoplan in moderate hepatic impairment subjects is not expected to be clinically significant. There was no meaningful difference identified with respect to total C5 levels and complement inhibition (sRBC lysis) between moderate hepatic impairment subjects and normal subjects despite a 24% lower zilucoplan systemic exposure in moderate hepatic impairment subjects. The review team agrees with the Applicant's conclusions and accordingly, no dose adjustment in subjects with mild and moderate hepatic impairment is required. For subjects with severe hepatic impairment, it will be proposed to the Applicant to include the following language in section 12.3 of labeling "The pharmacokinetics of zilucoplan in patients with severe hepatic impairment have not been studied."
L1055: Based on the concentrations of zilucoplan and metabolites in feces and the estimated fraction excreted by the fecal route, zilucoplan was minimally excreted in both the normal and in the moderate hepatic impairment subjects (&lt;1% of dose in total).
L1056: ## 14.2.5. Study # UP0115 – Relative Bioavailability Study (Impact of Injection Sites)
L1057: ## Study Design
L1058: Study UP0115 was a Phase 1, single-center, randomized, open-label, two-way incomplete cross over study in healthy subjects to assess the relative bioavailability of a single subcutaneous injection of zilucoplan (0.3 mg/kg) at three different sites (abdomen [reference site], thigh, and upper arm) and to evaluate the potential impact of different injection sites on PK and PD of zilucoplan. The incomplete crossover design allowed each subject to receive a single injection of zilucoplan in two of the three different injection sites. The study schematic is presented in Figure 5. There were eight subjects in each Group, A (abdomen/arm) and B (abdomen/thigh). The PK sampling time points were: predose and at 0.5, 1, 2, 3, 4, 6, 8, 12, and 24 hours postdose and on days 3, 5, 8, 15, 22, 29, and 35 postdose. The PD sampling time points for sRBC lysis were: predose and at 1, 3, 6, 24, and 96 hours postdose and on days 15 and 35 postdose.
L1059: **Figure 5. Study Schematic (Study # UP0115)**
L1060: EOS=End of Study; n=number of study participants
Source: Table 3-2 (Pg 29 of 97) from UP0115 Clinical Study Report

L1079: ## **Key Conclusions**
L1080: There were no notable differences in peak (Cmax) and systemic (AUC0-t) exposure of zilucoplan between abdomen and arm injection sites or between abdomen and thigh injection sites. For both Cmax and AUC0-t, the geometric least squares mean ratio for the comparison of arm versus abdomen and thigh versus abdomen was close to 1 with 90% CI within criteria of 80% to 125%.
L1081: The results for PD parameters of sRBC lysis also suggested the same conclusion. The above findings support the administration instruction in the labeling “Administer ZILBRYSQ subcutaneously into areas of the abdomen, thighs, or back of the upper arms.”
L1082: ## 14.3. Bioanalytical Method Validation and Performance
L1083: ## 14.3.1. Pharmacokinetic Bioanalytical Methods
L1084: Zilucoplan and metabolite (RA102758, and RA103488) exposures in plasma, urine, and feces (as applicable in relevant study) were determined using liquid chromatography mass spectrometry (LC-MS/MS). The LC-MS/MS methods as outlined in Table 84 were validated for their use in analyzing samples obtained from Phase 1, 2, and 3 clinical studies. These methods were validated for standard calibration curve and quality control (QC) performance (accuracy and precision); recovery; selectivity; matrix effect; interference and specificity; hemolysis effect; lipemic effect; dilution integrity; bench-top/process stability; freeze-thaw stability; long-term storage stability; stability in processed extract samples; stock solution stability; reinjection stability in extracts; whole blood stability; and carry over. The method performance during the study sample analysis was also evaluated by standard curve and QC performance and method reproducibility (incurred sample reanalysis) assessment.
